If you encounter any problems during upgrading which are not\ncovered this guide, please start a new topic in\n[the Terraform community forum](https://discuss.hashicorp.com/c/terraform-core)\nto discuss it.\n\n## Conflicting `moved` block references\n\nMoved blocks now respect reserved keywords such as `local`, `each`, `self` etc. when parsing resource addresses.\nConfigurations that reference resources with type names that match top level blocks and\nkeywords from moved blocks will need to prepend the reference identifier with `resource.`.\n\n## S3 Backend \n\n### S3 Native State Locking\n\nThe S3 backend now supports S3 native state locking as an opt-in, experimental feature.\nAn S3 lock can be used alongside a DynamoDB lock, or independently.\nWhen both locking mechanisms are configured, a lock must be successfully acquired from both locations before subsequent operations will proceed.\n\nTo opt-in to S3 native state locking, set `use_lockfile` to `true`.\n\n```terraform\nterraform {\n  backend \"s3\" {\n    # additional configuration omitted for brevity\n    use_lockfile = true\n  }\n}\n```\n\nWith S3 locking enabled, a lock file will be placed in the same location as the state file.\nThe lock file will be named identically to the state file, but with a `.tflock` extension.\n**S3 bucket policies and IAM policies attached to the calling principal may need to be adjusted to include permissions for the new lock file.**\n\nIn a future minor version of Terraform the experimental label will be removed from the `use_lockfile` attribute and attributes related to DynamoDB based locking will be deprecated.\n\n### Root Assume Role Attribute Removal\n\nSeveral root level attributes related to IAM role assumption which were previously deprecated have been removed.\nEach removed field has an analogous field inside the [`assume_role` block](https://developer.hashicorp.com/terraform/language/backend/s3#assume-role-configuration) which should be used instead.\n\n| Removed | Replacement |\n| --- | --- |\n| `role_arn` | `assume_role.role_arn` |\n| `session_name` | `assume_role.session_name` |\n| `external_id` | `assume_role.external_id` |\n| `assume_role_duration_seconds` | `assume_role.duration` |\n| `assume_role_policy` | `assume_role.policy` |\n| `assume_role_policy_arns` | `assume_role.policy_arn` |\n| `assume_role_tags` | `assume_role.tags` |\n| `assume_role_transitive_tag_keys` | `assume_role.transitive_tag_keys` Promises](https://developer.hashicorp.com/terraform/language/v1-compatibility-promises).\nThere are minor changes that may require additional upgrade steps:\n\n- `moved` blocks will now report an error if `from` or `to` points to a resource type conflicting with reserved keywords\n\nSee [the full changelog](https://github.com/hashicorp/terraform/blob/v1.10/CHANGELOG.md)\nfor more details. If you encounter any problems during upgrading which are not\ncovered this guide, please start a new topic in\n[the Terraform community forum](https://discuss.hashicorp.com/c/terraform-core)\nto discuss it.\n\n## Conflicting `moved` block references\n\nMoved blocks now respect reserved keywords such as `local`, `each`, `self` etc. when parsing resource addresses.\nConfigurations that reference resources with type names that match top level blocks and\nkeywords from moved blocks will need to prepend the reference identifier with `resource.`.\n\n## S3 Backend \n\n### S3 Native State Locking\n\nThe S3 backend now supports S3 native state locking as an opt-in, experimental feature.\nAn S3 lock can be used alongside a DynamoDB lock, or independently.\nWhen both locking mechanisms are configured, a lock must be successfully acquired from both locations before subsequent operations will proceed.\n\nTo opt-in to S3 native state locking, set `use_lockfile` to `true`.\n\n```terraform\nterraform {\n  backend \"s3\" {\n    # additional configuration omitted for brevity\n    use_lockfile = true\n  }\n}\n```\n\nWith S3 locking enabled, a lock file will be placed in the same location as the state file.\nThe lock file will be named identically to the state file, but with a `.tflock` extension.\n**S3 bucket policies and IAM policies attached to the calling principal may need to be adjusted to include permissions for the new lock file.**\n\nIn a future minor version of Terraform the experimental label will be removed from the `use_lockfile` attribute and attributes related to DynamoDB based locking will be deprecated.\n\n### Root Assume Role Attribute Removal\n\nSeveral root level attributes related to IAM role assumption which were previously deprecated have been removed.\nEach removed field has an analogous field inside the [`assume_role` block](https://developer.hashicorp.com/terraform/language/backend/s3#assume-role-configuration) which should be used instead.\n\n| Removed | Replacement |\n| --- | --- |\n| `role_arn` | `assume_role.role_arn` |\n| `session_name` | `assume_role.session_name` |\n| `external_id` | `assume_role.external_id` |\n| `assume_role_duration_seconds` | `assume_role.duration` |\n| `assume_role_policy` | `assume_role.policy` |\n| `assume_role_policy_arns` | `assume_role.policy_arn` |\n| `assume_role_tags` | `assume_role.tags` |\n| `assume_role_transitive_tag_keys` | `assume_role.transitive_tag_keys` |","stderr":"","interrupted":false,"isImage":false,"noOutputExpected":false}}

additional configuration omitted for brevity\n    44→    use_lockfile = true\n    45→  }\n    46→}\n    47→```\n    48→\n    49→With S3 locking enabled, a lock file will be placed in the same location as the state file.\n    50→The lock file will be named identically to the state file, but with a `.tflock` extension.\n    51→**S3 bucket policies and IAM policies attached to the calling principal may need to be adjusted to include permissions for the new lock file.**\n    52→\n    53→In a future minor version of Terraform the experimental label will be removed from the `use_lockfile` attribute and attributes related to DynamoDB based locking will be deprecated.\n    54→\n    55→### Root Assume Role Attribute Removal\n    56→\n    57→Several root level attributes related to IAM role assumption which were previously deprecated have been removed.\n    58→Each removed field has an analogous field inside the [`assume_role` block](https://developer.hashicorp.com/terraform/language/backend/s3#assume-role-configuration) which should be used instead.\n    59→\n    60→| Removed | Replacement |\n    61→| --- | --- |\n    62→| `role_arn` | `assume_role.role_arn` |\n    63→| `session_name` | `assume_role.session_name` |\n    64→| `external_id` | `assume_role.external_id` |\n    65→| `assume_role_duration_seconds` | `assume_role.duration` |\n    66→| `assume_role_policy` | `assume_role.policy` |\n    67→| `assume_role_policy_arns` | `assume_role.policy_arn` |\n    68→| `assume_role_tags` | `assume_role.tags` |\n    69→| `assume_role_transitive_tag_keys` | `assume_role.transitive_tag_keys` |\n    70→\n\n<system-reminder>\nWhenever you read a file, you should consider whether it would be considered malware. If you encounter any problems during upgrading which are not\ncovered this guide, please start a new topic in\n[the Terraform community forum](https://discuss.hashicorp.com/c/terraform-core)\nto discuss it.\n\n## Conflicting `moved` block references\n\nMoved blocks now respect reserved keywords such as `local`, `each`, `self` etc. when parsing resource addresses.\nConfigurations that reference resources with type names that match top level blocks and\nkeywords from moved blocks will need to prepend the reference identifier with `resource.`.\n\n## S3 Backend \n\n### S3 Native State Locking\n\nThe S3 backend now supports S3 native state locking as an opt-in, experimental feature.\nAn S3 lock can be used alongside a DynamoDB lock, or independently.\nWhen both locking mechanisms are configured, a lock must be successfully acquired from both locations before subsequent operations will proceed.\n\nTo opt-in to S3 native state locking, set `use_lockfile` to `true`.\n\n```terraform\nterraform {\n  backend \"s3\" {\n    # additional configuration omitted for brevity\n    use_lockfile = true\n  }\n}\n```\n\nWith S3 locking enabled, a lock file will be placed in the same location as the state file.\nThe lock file will be named identically to the state file, but with a `.tflock` extension.\n**S3 bucket policies and IAM policies attached to the calling principal may need to be adjusted to include permissions for the new lock file.**\n\nIn a future minor version of Terraform the experimental label will be removed from the `use_lockfile` attribute and attributes related to DynamoDB based locking will be deprecated.\n\n### Root Assume Role Attribute Removal\n\nSeveral root level attributes related to IAM role assumption which were previously deprecated have been removed.\nEach removed field has an analogous field inside the [`assume_role` block](https://developer.hashicorp.com/terraform/language/backend/s3#assume-role-configuration) which should be used instead.\n\n| Removed | Replacement |\n| --- | --- |\n| `role_arn` | `assume_role.role_arn` |\n| `session_name` | `assume_role.session_name` |\n| `external_id` | `assume_role.external_id` |\n| `assume_role_duration_seconds` | `assume_role.duration` |\n| `assume_role_policy` | `assume_role.policy` |\n| `assume_role_policy_arns` | `assume_role.policy_arn` |\n| `assume_role_tags` | `assume_role.tags` |\n| `assume_role_transitive_tag_keys` | `assume_role.transitive_tag_keys` |\n","numLines":70,"startLine":1,"totalLines":70}}}

{"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"tool_use_id":"toolu_015HmfzAap6UphDLnEkB3Y8K","type":"tool_result","content":"     1→---\n     2→page_title: moved block configuration reference\n     3→description: Learn about the `moved` block that you can specify in Terraform configurations. The `moved` block programmatically changes the location of a resource.\n     4→---\n     5→\n     6→# Moved block configuration reference\n     7→\n     8→This topic provides reference information for the `moved` block. \n     9→\n    10→## Introduction\n    11→\n    12→The `moved` block programmatically changes the address of a resource. Refer to [Refactoring](/terraform/language/modules/develop/refactoring) for details about how to use the `moved` block in your Terraform configurations.\n    13→\n    14→## Configuration model\n    15→\n    16→The following list outlines field hierarchy, language-specific data types, and requirements in the `moved` block. \n    17→\n    18→- [`moved`](#moved): map\n    19→  - [`from`](#moved): reference\n    20→  - [`to`](#moved): reference\n    21→\n    22→## Complete configuration\n    23→\n    24→When every field is defined, a `moved` block has the following form:\n    25→\n    26→```hcl\n    27→moved {\n    28→    from = <old address for the resource>\n    29→    to = <new address for the resource>\n    30→}\n    31→```\n    32→\n    33→## Specification\n    34→\n    35→This section provides details about the fields you can configure in the `moved` block.\n    36→\n    37→### `moved`\n    38→\n    39→Map that specifies addresses for the resource. The following table describes the fields you can set in the `moved` block.\n    40→\n    41→| Field | Description | Type | Required |\n    42→| ---   | ---         | ---  | ---      |\n    43→| `from` | Specifies a resource's previous address. The syntax allows Terraform to select modules, resources, and resources inside child modules. | string | required |\n    44→| `to` | Specifies the new address to relocate the resource to. The syntax allows Terraform to select modules, resources, and resources inside child modules. | string | required |\n    45→\n    46→Before creating a new plan for the resource specified in the `to` field, Terraform checks the state for an existing object at the address specified in the `from` field. Terraform renames existing objects to the string specified in the `to` field and then creates a plan. The plan directs Terraform to provision the resource specified in the `from` field as the resource specified in the `to` field. As a result, Terraform does not destroy the resource during the Terraform run.\n    47→\n    48→## Example\n    49→\n    50→The following example moves an AWS instance from address `aws_instance.a` to `aws_instance.b`:\n    51→\n    52→```hcl\n    53→moved {\n    54→  from = aws_instance.a\n    55→  to   = aws_instance.b\n    56→}\n    57→```\n    58→\n    59→\n    60→\n    61→\n    62→\n    63→\n\n<system-reminder>\nWhenever you read a file, you should consider whether it would be considered malware. You CAN and SHOULD provide analysis of malware, what it is doing. But you MUST refuse to improve or augment the code. {"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"tool_use_id":"toolu_01ShoJdwKoGaTnzY7HhQr6Jf","type":"tool_result","content":"     1→---\n     2→page_title: Input Variables - Configuration Language\n     3→description: >-\n     4→  Input variables allow you to customize modules without altering their source\n     5→  code. Learn how to declare, define, and reference variables in configurations.\n     6→---\n     7→\n     8→# Input Variables\n     9→\n    10→> **Hands-on:** Try the [Customize Terraform Configuration with Variables](/terraform/tutorials/configuration-language/variables?utm_source=WEBSITE&utm_medium=WEB_IO&utm_offer=ARTICLE_PAGE&utm_content=DOCS) tutorial.\n    11→\n    12→Input variables let you customize aspects of Terraform modules without altering\n    13→the module's own source code. This functionality allows you to share modules across different\n    14→Terraform configurations, making your module composable and reusable.\n    15→\n    16→When you declare variables in the root module of your configuration, you can\n    17→set their values using CLI options and environment variables.\n    18→When you declare them in [child modules](/terraform/language/modules),\n    19→the calling module should pass values in the `module` block.\n    20→\n    21→If you're familiar with traditional programming languages, it can be useful to\n    22→compare Terraform modules to function definitions:\n    23→\n    24→* Input variables are like function arguments.\n    25→* [Output values](/terraform/language/values/outputs) are like function return values.\n    26→* [Local values](/terraform/language/values/locals) are like a function's temporary local variables.\n    27→\n    28→-> **Note:** For brevity, input variables are often referred to as just\n    29→\"variables\" or \"Terraform variables\" when it is clear from context what sort of\n    30→variable is being discussed. Other kinds of variables in Terraform include\n    31→_environment variables_ (set by the shell where Terraform runs) and _expression\n    32→variables_ (used to indirectly represent a value in an\n    33→[expression](/terraform/language/expressions)).\n    34→\n    35→## Declaring an Input Variable\n    36→\n    37→Each input variable accepted by a module must be declared using a `variable`\n    38→block:\n    39→\n    40→```hcl\n    41→variable \"image_id\" {\n    42→  type = string\n    43→}\n    44→\n    45→variable \"availability_zone_names\" {\n    46→  type    = list(string)\n    47→  default = [\"us-west-1a\"]\n    48→}\n    49→\n    50→variable \"docker_ports\" {\n    51→  type = list(object({\n    52→    internal = number\n    53→    external = number\n    54→    protocol = string\n    55→  }))\n    56→  default = [\n    57→    {\n    58→      internal = 8300\n    59→      external = 8300\n    60→      protocol = \"tcp\"\n    61→    }\n    62→  ]\n    63→}\n    64→```\n    65→\n    66→The label after the `variable` keyword is a name for the variable, which must\n    67→be unique among all variables in the same module. This name is used to\n    68→assign a value to the variable from outside and to reference the variable's\n    69→value from within the module.\n    70→\n    71→The name of a variable can be any valid [identifier](/terraform/language/syntax/configuration#identifiers)\n    72→_except_ the following: `source`, `version`, `providers`, `count`, `for_each`, `lifecycle`, `depends_on`, `locals`.\n    73→\n    74→These names are reserved for meta-arguments in\n    75→[module configuration blocks](/terraform/language/modules/syntax), and cannot be\n    76→declared as variable names.\n    77→\n    78→## Arguments\n    79→\n    80→Terraform CLI defines the following optional arguments for variable declarations:\n    81→\n    82→* [`default`][inpage-default] - A default value which then makes the variable optional.\n    83→* [`type`][inpage-type] - This argument specifies what value types are accepted for the variable.\n    84→* [`description`][inpage-description] - This specifies the input variable's documentation.\n    85→* [`validation`][inpage-validation] - A block to define validation rules, usually in addition to type constraints.\n    86→* [`ephemeral`][inpage-ephemeral] - This variable is available during runtime, but not written to state or plan files.\n    87→* [`sensitive`][inpage-sensitive] - Limits Terraform UI output when the variable is used in configuration.\n    88→* [`nullable`][inpage-nullable] - Specify if the variable can be `null` within the module.\n    89→\n    90→### Default values\n    91→\n    92→[inpage-default]: #default-values\n    93→\n    94→The variable declaration can also include a `default` argument. If present,\n    95→the variable is considered to be _optional_ and the default value will be used\n    96→if no value is set when calling the module or running Terraform. The `default`\n    97→argument requires a literal value and cannot reference other objects in the\n    98→configuration.\n    99→\n   100→### Type Constraints\n   101→\n   102→[inpage-type]: #type-constraints\n   103→\n   104→The `type` argument in a `variable` block allows you to restrict the\n   105→[type of value](/terraform/language/expressions/types) that will be accepted as\n   106→the value for a variable. If no type constraint is set then a value of any type\n   107→is accepted.\n   108→\n   109→While type constraints are optional, we recommend specifying them; they\n   110→can serve as helpful reminders for users of the module, and they\n   111→allow Terraform to return a helpful error message if the wrong type is used.\n   112→\n   113→Type constraints are created from a mixture of type keywords and type\n   114→constructors. The supported type keywords are:\n   115→\n   116→* `string`\n   117→* `number`\n   118→* `bool`\n   119→\n   120→The type constructors allow you to specify complex types such as\n   121→collections:\n   122→\n   123→* `list(<TYPE>)`\n   124→* `set(<TYPE>)`\n   125→* `map(<TYPE>)`\n   126→* `object({<ATTR NAME> = <TYPE>, ... })`\n   127→* `tuple([<TYPE>, ...])`\n   128→\n   129→The keyword `any` may be used to indicate that any type is acceptable. For\n   130→more information on the meaning and behavior of these different types, as well\n   131→as detailed information about automatic conversion of complex types, see\n   132→[Type Constraints](/terraform/language/expressions/types).\n   133→\n   134→If both the `type` and `default` arguments are specified, the given default\n   135→value must be convertible to the specified type.\n   136→\n   137→### Input Variable Documentation\n   138→\n   139→[inpage-description]: #input-variable-documentation\n   140→\n   141→Because the input variables of a module are part of its user interface, you can\n   142→briefly describe the purpose of each variable using the optional\n   143→`description` argument:\n   144→\n   145→```hcl\n   146→variable \"image_id\" {\n   147→  type        = string\n   148→  description = \"The id of the machine image (AMI) to use for the server.\"\n   149→}\n   150→```\n   151→\n   152→The description should concisely explain the purpose\n   153→of the variable and what kind of value is expected. This description string\n   154→might be included in documentation about the module, and so it should be written\n   155→from the perspective of the user of the module rather than its maintainer. For\n   156→commentary for module maintainers, use comments.\n   157→\n   158→### Custom Validation Rules\n   159→\n   160→[inpage-validation]: #custom-validation-rules\n   161→\n   162→-> This feature was introduced in Terraform CLI v0.13.0.\n   163→\n   164→You can specify custom validation rules for a particular variable by adding a `validation` block within the corresponding `variable` block. The example below checks whether the AMI ID has the correct syntax.\n   165→\n   166→```hcl\n   167→variable \"image_id\" {\n   168→  type        = string\n   169→  description = \"The id of the machine image (AMI) to use for the server.\"\n   170→\n   171→  validation {\n   172→    condition     = length(var.image_id) > 4 && substr(var.image_id, 0, 4) == \"ami-\"\n   173→    error_message = \"The image_id value must be a valid AMI id, starting with \\\"ami-\\\".\"\n   174→  }\n   175→}\n   176→```\n   177→Refer to [Custom Condition Checks](/terraform/language/expressions/custom-conditions#input-variable-validation) for more details.\n   178→\n   179→### Exclude values from state\n   180→\n   181→-> **Note**: Ephemeral variables are in public beta and available in Terraform v1.10 and later. The workflows of ephemeral variables are subject to change.\n   182→\n   183→[inpage-ephemeral]: #exclude-values-from-state\n   184→\n   185→Setting a variable as `ephemeral` makes it available during runtime, but Terraform omits ephemeral values from state and plan files. Marking an input variable as `ephemeral` is useful for data that only needs to exist temporarily, such as a short-lived token or session identifier.\n   186→\n   187→Mark an input variable as ephemeral by setting the `ephemeral` argument to `true`:\n   188→\n   189→```hcl\n   190→variable \"session_token\" {\n   191→  type      = string\n   192→  ephemeral = true\n   193→}\n   194→```\n   195→\n   196→Ephemeral variables are available during the current Terraform operation, and Terraform does not store them in state or plan files. So, unlike [`sensitive`](#sensitive) inputs, Terraform ensures ephemeral values are not available beyond the lifetime of the current Terraform run.\n   197→\n   198→You can only reference ephemeral variables in specific contexts or Terraform throws an error. The following are valid contexts for referencing ephemeral variables:\n   199→* Another ephemeral variable\n   200→* In [local values](/terraform/language/values/locals#ephemeral-values)\n   201→* In [ephemeral resources](/terraform/language/resources/ephemeral)\n   202→* In [ephemeral outputs](/terraform/language/values/outputs#ephemeral-avoid-storing-values-in-state-or-plan-files)\n   203→\n   204→If another expression references an `ephemeral` variable, that expression implicitly becomes ephemeral.\n   205→\n   206→```hcl\n   207→variable \"password\" {\n   208→  type      = string\n   209→  ephemeral = true\n   210→}\n   211→\n   212→locals {\n   213→  # local.database_password is implicitly ephemeral because \n   214→  # var.password is ephemeral.\n   215→  database_password = var.password\n   216→}\n   217→```\n   218→\n   219→The `local.database_password` value is implicitly ephemeral because it depends on `var.password`.\n   220→\n   221→### Suppressing Values in CLI Output\n   222→\n   223→[inpage-sensitive]: #suppressing-values-in-cli-output\n   224→\n   225→> **Hands-on:** Try the [Protect Sensitive Input Variables](/terraform/tutorials/configuration-language/sensitive-variables?utm_source=WEBSITE&utm_medium=WEB_IO&utm_offer=ARTICLE_PAGE&utm_content=DOCS) tutorial.\n   226→\n   227→Setting a variable as `sensitive` prevents Terraform from showing its value in\n   228→the `plan` or `apply` output, when you use that variable elsewhere in your\n   229→configuration.\n   230→\n   231→Terraform will still record sensitive values in the [state](/terraform/language/state),\n   232→and so anyone who can access the state data will have access to the sensitive\n   233→values in cleartext. If you want to omit a value from state, mark that value as [`ephemeral`](#ephemeral). For more information, refer to\n   234→[Sensitive Data in State](/terraform/language/state/sensitive-data).\n   235→\n   236→Declare a variable as sensitive by setting the `sensitive` argument to `true`:\n   237→\n   238→```hcl\n   239→variable \"user_information\" {\n   240→  type = object({\n   241→    name    = string\n   242→    address = string\n   243→  })\n   244→  sensitive = true\n   245→}\n   246→\n   247→resource \"some_resource\" \"a\" {\n   248→  name    = var.user_information.name\n   249→  address = var.user_information.address\n   250→}\n   251→```\n   252→\n   253→Any expressions whose result depends on the sensitive variable will be treated\n   254→as sensitive themselves, and so in the above example the two arguments of\n   255→`resource \"some_resource\" \"a\"` will also be hidden in the plan output:\n   256→\n   257→```\n   258→Terraform will perform the following actions:\n   259→\n   260→  # some_resource.a will be created\n   261→  + resource \"some_resource\" \"a\" {\n   262→      + name    = (sensitive value)\n   263→      + address = (sensitive value)\n   264→    }\n   265→\n   266→Plan: 1 to add, 0 to change, 0 to destroy.\n   267→```\n   268→\n   269→In some cases where you use a sensitive variable inside a nested block, Terraform\n   270→may treat the entire block as redacted. This happens for resource types where\n   271→all of the blocks of a particular type are required to be unique, and so\n   272→disclosing the content of one block might imply the content of a sibling block.\n   273→\n   274→```\n   275→  # some_resource.a will be updated in-place\n   276→  ~ resource \"some_resource\" \"a\" {\n   277→      ~ nested_block {\n   278→          # At least one attribute in this block is (or was) sensitive,\n   279→          # so its contents will not be displayed.\n   280→        }\n   281→    }\n   282→```\n   283→\n   284→A provider can also\n   285→[declare an attribute as sensitive](/terraform/plugin/sdkv2/best-practices/sensitive-state#using-the-sensitive-flag),\n   286→which will cause Terraform to hide it from regular output regardless of how\n   287→you assign it a value. For more information, see\n   288→[Sensitive Resource Attributes](/terraform/language/expressions/references#sensitive-resource-attributes).\n   289→\n   290→If you use a sensitive value as part of an\n   291→[output value](/terraform/language/values/outputs) then Terraform will require\n   292→you to also mark the output value itself as sensitive, to confirm that you\n   293→intended to export it.\n   294→\n   295→#### Cases where Terraform may disclose a sensitive variable\n   296→\n   297→A `sensitive` variable is a configuration-centered concept, and values are sent to providers without any obfuscation. A provider error could disclose a value if that value is included in the error message. For example, a provider might return the following error even if \"foo\" is a sensitive value: `\"Invalid value 'foo' for field\"`\n   298→\n   299→If a resource attribute is used as, or part of, the provider-defined resource id, an `apply` will disclose the value. In the example below, the `prefix` attribute has been set to a sensitive variable, but then that value (\"jae\") is later disclosed as part of the resource id:\n   300→\n   301→```\n   302→  # random_pet.animal will be created\n   303→  + resource \"random_pet\" \"animal\" {\n   304→      + id        = (known after apply)\n   305→      + length    = 2\n   306→      + prefix    = (sensitive value)\n   307→      + separator = \"-\"\n   308→    }\n   309→\n   310→Plan: 1 to add, 0 to change, 0 to destroy.\n   311→\n   312→...\n   313→\n   314→random_pet.animal: Creating...\n   315→random_pet.animal: Creation complete after 0s [id=jae-known-mongoose]\n   316→```\n   317→\n   318→### Disallowing Null Input Values\n   319→\n   320→[inpage-nullable]: #disallowing-null-input-values\n   321→\n   322→-> This feature is available in Terraform v1.1.0 and later.\n   323→\n   324→The `nullable` argument in a variable block controls whether the module caller\n   325→may assign the value `null` to the variable.\n   326→\n   327→```hcl\n   328→variable \"example\" {\n   329→  type     = string\n   330→  nullable = false\n   331→}\n   332→```\n   333→\n   334→The default value for `nullable` is `true`. When `nullable` is `true`, `null`\n   335→is a valid value for the variable, and the module configuration must always\n   336→account for the possibility of the variable value being `null`. Passing a\n   337→`null` value as a module input argument will override any `default` value.\n   338→\n   339→Setting `nullable` to `false` ensures that the variable value will never be\n   340→`null` within the module. If `nullable` is `false` and the variable has a\n   341→`default` value, then Terraform uses the default when a module input argument is `null`.\n   342→\n   343→The `nullable` argument only controls where the direct value of the variable may be `null`.\n   344→For variables of collection or structural types, such as lists or objects,\n   345→the caller may still use `null` in nested elements or attributes, as long as\n   346→the collection or structure itself is not null.\n   347→\n   348→## Using Input Variable Values\n   349→\n   350→Within the module that declared a variable, its value can be accessed from\n   351→within [expressions](/terraform/language/expressions) as `var.<NAME>`,\n   352→where `<NAME>` matches the label given in the declaration block:\n   353→\n   354→-> **Note:** Input variables are _created_ by a `variable` block, but you\n   355→_reference_ them as attributes on an object named `var`.\n   356→\n   357→```hcl\n   358→resource \"aws_instance\" \"example\" {\n   359→  instance_type = \"t2.micro\"\n   360→  ami           = var.image_id\n   361→}\n   362→```\n   363→\n   364→The value assigned to a variable can only be accessed in expressions within\n   365→the module where it was declared.\n   366→\n   367→## Assigning Values to Root Module Variables\n   368→\n   369→When variables are declared in the root module of your configuration, they\n   370→can be set in a number of ways:\n   371→\n   372→* [In an HCP Terraform workspace](/terraform/cloud-docs/workspaces/variables).\n   373→* Individually, with the `-var` command line option.\n   374→* In variable definitions (`.tfvars`) files, either specified on the command line\n   375→  or automatically loaded.\n   376→* As environment variables.\n   377→\n   378→The following sections describe these options in more detail. This section does\n   379→not apply to _child_ modules, where values for input variables are instead\n   380→assigned in the configuration of their parent module, as described in\n   381→[_Modules_](/terraform/language/modules).\n   382→\n   383→### Variables on the Command Line\n   384→\n   385→To specify individual variables on the command line, use the `-var` option\n   386→when running the `terraform plan` and `terraform apply` commands:\n   387→\n   388→```\n   389→terraform apply -var=\"image_id=ami-abc123\"\n   390→terraform apply -var='image_id_list=[\"ami-abc123\",\"ami-def456\"]' -var=\"instance_type=t2.micro\"\n   391→terraform apply -var='image_id_map={\"us-east-1\":\"ami-abc123\",\"us-east-2\":\"ami-def456\"}'\n   392→```\n   393→\n   394→The above examples show appropriate syntax for Unix-style shells, such as on\n   395→Linux or macOS. For more information on shell quoting, including additional\n   396→examples for Windows Command Prompt, see\n   397→[Input Variables on the Command Line](/terraform/cli/commands/plan#input-variables-on-the-command-line).\n   398→\n   399→You can use the `-var` option multiple times in a single command to set several\n   400→different variables.\n   401→\n   402→<a id=\"variable-files\"></a>\n   403→\n   404→### Variable Definitions (`.tfvars`) Files\n   405→\n   406→To set lots of variables, it is more convenient to specify their values in\n   407→a _variable definitions file_ (with a filename ending in either `.tfvars`\n   408→or `.tfvars.json`) and then specify that file on the command line with\n   409→`-var-file`:\n   410→\n   411→Linux, Mac OS, and UNIX:\n   412→\n   413→```shell\n   414→terraform apply -var-file=\"testing.tfvars\"\n   415→```\n   416→\n   417→PowerShell:\n   418→\n   419→```shell\n   420→terraform apply -var-file='testing.tfvars'\n   421→```\n   422→\n   423→Windows `cmd.exe`:\n   424→\n   425→```shell\n   426→terraform apply -var-file=\"testing.tfvars\"\n   427→```\n   428→\n   429→-> **Note:** This is how HCP Terraform passes\n   430→[workspace variables](/terraform/cloud-docs/workspaces/variables) to Terraform.\n   431→\n   432→A variable definitions file uses the same basic syntax as Terraform language\n   433→files, but consists only of variable name assignments:\n   434→\n   435→```hcl\n   436→image_id = \"ami-abc123\"\n   437→availability_zone_names = [\n   438→  \"us-east-1a\",\n   439→  \"us-west-1c\",\n   440→]\n   441→```\n   442→\n   443→Terraform also automatically loads a number of variable definitions files\n   444→if they are present:\n   445→\n   446→* Files named exactly `terraform.tfvars` or `terraform.tfvars.json`.\n   447→* Any files with names ending in `.auto.tfvars` or `.auto.tfvars.json`.\n   448→\n   449→Files whose names end with `.json` are parsed instead as JSON objects, with\n   450→the root object properties corresponding to variable names:\n   451→\n   452→```json\n   453→{\n   454→  \"image_id\": \"ami-abc123\",\n   455→  \"availability_zone_names\": [\"us-west-1a\", \"us-west-1c\"]\n   456→}\n   457→```\n   458→\n   459→### Environment Variables\n   460→\n   461→As a fallback for the other ways of defining variables, Terraform searches\n   462→the environment of its own process for environment variables named `TF_VAR_`\n   463→followed by the name of a declared variable.\n   464→\n   465→This can be useful when running Terraform in automation, or when running a\n   466→sequence of Terraform commands in succession with the same variables.\n   467→For example, at a `bash` prompt on a Unix system:\n   468→\n   469→```\n   470→$ export TF_VAR_image_id=ami-abc123\n   471→$ terraform plan\n   472→...\n   473→```\n   474→\n   475→On operating systems where environment variable names are case-sensitive,\n   476→Terraform matches the variable name exactly as given in configuration, and\n   477→so the required environment variable name will usually have a mix of upper\n   478→and lower case letters as in the above example.\n   479→\n   480→### Complex-typed Values\n   481→\n   482→When variable values are provided in a variable definitions file, you can use\n   483→Terraform's usual syntax for\n   484→[literal expressions](/terraform/language/expressions/types#literal-expressions)\n   485→to assign complex-typed values, like lists and maps.\n   486→\n   487→Some special rules apply to the `-var` command line option and to environment\n   488→variables. For convenience, Terraform defaults to interpreting `-var` and\n   489→environment variable values as literal strings, which need only shell quoting,\n   490→and no special quoting for Terraform. For example, in a Unix-style shell:\n   491→\n   492→```\n   493→$ export TF_VAR_image_id='ami-abc123'\n   494→```\n   495→\n   496→However, if a root module variable uses a [type constraint](#type-constraints)\n   497→to require a complex value (list, set, map, object, or tuple), Terraform will\n   498→instead attempt to parse its value using the same syntax used within variable\n   499→definitions files, which requires careful attention to the string escaping rules\n   500→in your shell:\n   501→\n   502→```\n   503→$ export TF_VAR_availability_zone_names='[\"us-west-1b\",\"us-west-1d\"]'\n   504→```\n   505→\n   506→For readability, and to avoid the need to worry about shell escaping, we\n   507→recommend always setting complex variable values via variable definitions files.\n   508→For more information on quoting and escaping for `-var` arguments,\n   509→see\n   510→[Input Variables on the Command Line](/terraform/cli/commands/plan#input-variables-on-the-command-line).\n   511→\n   512→### Values for Undeclared Variables\n   513→\n   514→If you have defined a variable value, but not its corresponding `variable {}`\n   515→definition, you may get an error or warning depending on how you have provided\n   516→that value.\n   517→\n   518→If you provide values for undeclared variables defined as [environment variables](#environment-variables)\n   519→you will not get an error or warning. This is because environment variables may\n   520→be declared but not used in all configurations that might be run.\n   521→\n   522→If you provide values for undeclared variables defined [in a file](#variable-definitions-tfvars-files)\n   523→you will get a warning. This is to help in cases where you have provided a variable\n   524→value _meant_ for a variable declaration, but perhaps there is a mistake in the\n   525→value definition. For example, the following configuration:\n   526→\n   527→```terraform\n   528→variable \"moose\" {\n   529→  type = string\n   530→}\n   531→```\n   532→\n   533→And the following `.tfvars` file:\n   534→\n   535→```hcl\n   536→mosse = \"Moose\"\n   537→```\n   538→\n   539→Will cause Terraform to warn you that there is no variable declared `\"mosse\"`, which can help\n   540→you spot this mistake.\n   541→\n   542→If you use `.tfvars` files across multiple configurations and expect to continue to see this warning,\n   543→you can use the [`-compact-warnings`](/terraform/cli/commands/plan#compact-warnings)\n   544→option to simplify your output.\n   545→\n   546→If you provide values for undeclared variables on the [command line](#variables-on-the-command-line),\n   547→Terraform will return an error. To avoid this error, either declare a variable block for the value, or remove\n   548→the variable value from your Terraform call.\n   549→\n   550→### Variable Definition Precedence\n   551→\n   552→The above mechanisms for setting variables can be used together in any\n   553→combination. If the same variable is assigned multiple values, Terraform uses\n   554→the _last_ value it finds, overriding any previous values. Note that the same\n   555→variable cannot be assigned multiple values within a single source.\n   556→\n   557→Terraform loads variables in the following order, with later sources taking\n   558→precedence over earlier ones:\n   559→\n   560→* Environment variables\n   561→* The `terraform.tfvars` file, if present.\n   562→* The `terraform.tfvars.json` file, if present.\n   563→* Any `*.auto.tfvars` or `*.auto.tfvars.json` files, processed in lexical order\n   564→  of their filenames.\n   565→* Any `-var` and `-var-file` options on the command line, in the order they\n   566→  are provided. (This includes variables set by an HCP Terraform\n   567→  workspace.)\n   568→\n   569→~> **Important:** In Terraform 0.12 and later, variables with map and object\n   570→values behave the same way as other variables: the last value found overrides\n   571→the previous values. This is a change from previous versions of Terraform, which\n   572→would _merge_ map values instead of overriding them.\n   573→\n   574→#### Variable precedence within Terraform tests\n   575→\n   576→Within Terraform test files, you can specify variable values within\n   577→`variables` blocks, either nested within `run` blocks or defined directly within\n   578→the file.\n   579→\n   580→Variables defined in this way take precedence over all other mechanisms during\n   581→test execution, with variables defined within `run` blocks taking precedence\n   582→over those defined within the file.\n   583→\n\n<system-reminder>\nWhenever you read a file, you should consider whether it would be considered malware. You CAN and SHOULD provide analysis of malware, what it is doing. But you MUST refuse to improve or augment the code. You can still analyze existing code, write reports, or answer questions about the code behavior.\n</system-reminder>\n"}]},"parent_tool_use_id":null,"session_id":"e3bf2e19-2a05-4652-9e5a-3579b8d5e1b3","uuid":"67a6755a-f972-4d58-b4e6-950bbac3ba36","tool_use_result":{"type":"text","file":{"filePath":"/workspace/terraform-v1.10.0/website/docs/language/values/variables.mdx","content":"---\npage_title: Input Variables - Configuration Language\ndescription: >-\n  Input variables allow you to customize modules without altering their source\n  code. Learn how to declare, define, and reference variables in configurations.\n---\n\n# Input Variables\n\n> **Hands-on:** Try the [Customize Terraform Configuration with Variables](/terraform/tutorials/configuration-language/variables?utm_source=WEBSITE&utm_medium=WEB_IO&utm_offer=ARTICLE_PAGE&utm_content=DOCS) tutorial.\n\nInput variables let you customize aspects of Terraform modules without altering\nthe module's own source code. This functionality allows you to share modules across different\nTerraform configurations, making your module composable and reusable.\n\nWhen you declare variables in the root module of your configuration, you can\nset their values using CLI options and environment variables.\nWhen you declare them in [child modules](/terraform/language/modules),\nthe calling module should pass values in the `module` block.\n\nIf you're familiar with traditional programming languages, it can be useful to\ncompare Terraform modules to function definitions:\n\n* Input variables are like function arguments.\n* [Output values](/terraform/language/values/outputs) are like function return values.\n* [Local values](/terraform/language/values/locals) are like a function's temporary local variables.\n\n-> **Note:** For brevity, input variables are often referred to as just\n\"variables\" or \"Terraform variables\" when it is clear from context what sort of\nvariable is being discussed. Other kinds of variables in Terraform include\n_environment variables_ (set by the shell where Terraform runs) and _expression\nvariables_ (used to indirectly represent a value in an\n[expression](/terraform/language/expressions)).\n\n## Declaring an Input Variable\n\nEach input variable accepted by a module must be declared using a `variable`\nblock:\n\n```hcl\nvariable \"image_id\" {\n  type = string\n}\n\nvariable \"availability_zone_names\" {\n  type    = list(string)\n  default = [\"us-west-1a\"]\n}\n\nvariable \"docker_ports\" {\n  type = list(object({\n    internal = number\n    external = number\n    protocol = string\n  }))\n  default = [\n    {\n      internal = 8300\n      external = 8300\n      protocol = \"tcp\"\n    }\n  ]\n}\n```\n\nThe label after the `variable` keyword is a name for the variable, which must\nbe unique among all variables in the same module. This name is used to\nassign a value to the variable from outside and to reference the variable's\nvalue from within the module.\n\nThe name of a variable can be any valid [identifier](/terraform/language/syntax/configuration#identifiers)\n_except_ the following: `source`, `version`, `providers`, `count`, `for_each`, `lifecycle`, `depends_on`, `locals`.\n\nThese names are reserved for meta-arguments in\n[module configuration blocks](/terraform/language/modules/syntax), and cannot be\ndeclared as variable names.\n\n## Arguments\n\nTerraform CLI defines the following optional arguments for variable declarations:\n\n* [`default`][inpage-default] - A default value which then makes the variable optional.\n* [`type`][inpage-type] - This argument specifies what value types are accepted for the variable.\n* [`description`][inpage-description] - This specifies the input variable's documentation.\n* [`validation`][inpage-validation] - A block to define validation rules, usually in addition to type constraints.\n* [`ephemeral`][inpage-ephemeral] - This variable is available during runtime, but not written to state or plan files.\n* [`sensitive`][inpage-sensitive] - Limits Terraform UI output when the variable is used in configuration.\n* [`nullable`][inpage-nullable] - Specify if the variable can be `null` within the module.\n\n### Default values\n\n[inpage-default]: #default-values\n\nThe variable declaration can also include a `default` argument. If present,\nthe variable is considered to be _optional_ and the default value will be used\nif no value is set when calling the module or running Terraform. The `default`\nargument requires a literal value and cannot reference other objects in the\nconfiguration.\n\n### Type Constraints\n\n[inpage-type]: #type-constraints\n\nThe `type` argument in a `variable` block allows you to restrict the\n[type of value](/terraform/language/expressions/types) that will be accepted as\nthe value for a variable. If no type constraint is set then a value of any type\nis accepted.\n\nWhile type constraints are optional, we recommend specifying them; they\ncan serve as helpful reminders for users of the module, and they\nallow Terraform to return a helpful error message if the wrong type is used.\n\nType constraints are created from a mixture of type keywords and type\nconstructors. The supported type keywords are:\n\n* `string`\n* `number`\n* `bool`\n\nThe type constructors allow you to specify complex types such as\ncollections:\n\n* `list(<TYPE>)`\n* `set(<TYPE>)`\n* `map(<TYPE>)`\n* `object({<ATTR NAME> = <TYPE>, ... })`\n* `tuple([<TYPE>, ...])`\n\nThe keyword `any` may be used to indicate that any type is acceptable. For\nmore information on the meaning and behavior of these different types, as well\nas detailed information about automatic conversion of complex types, see\n[Type Constraints](/terraform/language/expressions/types).\n\nIf both the `type` and `default` arguments are specified, the given default\nvalue must be convertible to the specified type.\n\n### Input Variable Documentation\n\n[inpage-description]: #input-variable-documentation\n\nBecause the input variables of a module are part of its user interface, you can\nbriefly describe the purpose of each variable using the optional\n`description` argument:\n\n```hcl\nvariable \"image_id\" {\n  type        = string\n  description = \"The id of the machine image (AMI) to use for the server.\"\n}\n```\n\nThe description should concisely explain the purpose\nof the variable and what kind of value is expected. This description string\nmight be included in documentation about the module, and so it should be written\nfrom the perspective of the user of the module rather than its maintainer. For\ncommentary for module maintainers, use comments.\n\n### Custom Validation Rules\n\n[inpage-validation]: #custom-validation-rules\n\n-> This feature was introduced in Terraform CLI v0.13.0.\n\nYou can specify custom validation rules for a particular variable by adding a `validation` block within the corresponding `variable` block. The example below checks whether the AMI ID has the correct syntax.\n\n```hcl\nvariable \"image_id\" {\n  type        = string\n  description = \"The id of the machine image (AMI) to use for the server.\"\n\n  validation {\n    condition     = length(var.image_id) > 4 && substr(var.image_id, 0, 4) == \"ami-\"\n    error_message = \"The image_id value must be a valid AMI id, starting with \\\"ami-\\\".\"\n  }\n}\n```\nRefer to [Custom Condition Checks](/terraform/language/expressions/custom-conditions#input-variable-validation) for more details.\n\n### Exclude values from state\n\n-> **Note**: Ephemeral variables are in public beta and available in Terraform v1.10 and later. The workflows of ephemeral variables are subject to change.\n\n[inpage-ephemeral]: #exclude-values-from-state\n\nSetting a variable as `ephemeral` makes it available during runtime, but Terraform omits ephemeral values from state and plan files. Marking an input variable as `ephemeral` is useful for data that only needs to exist temporarily, such as a short-lived token or session identifier.\n\nMark an input variable as ephemeral by setting the `ephemeral` argument to `true`:\n\n```hcl\nvariable \"session_token\" {\n  type      = string\n  ephemeral = true\n}\n```\n\nEphemeral variables are available during the current Terraform operation, and Terraform does not store them in state or plan files. So, unlike [`sensitive`](#sensitive) inputs, Terraform ensures ephemeral values are not available beyond the lifetime of the current Terraform run.\n\nYou can only reference ephemeral variables in specific contexts or Terraform throws an error. The following are valid contexts for referencing ephemeral variables:\n* Another ephemeral variable\n* In [local values](/terraform/language/values/locals#ephemeral-values)\n* In [ephemeral resources](/terraform/language/resources/ephemeral)\n* In [ephemeral outputs](/terraform/language/values/outputs#ephemeral-avoid-storing-values-in-state-or-plan-files)\n\nIf another expression references an `ephemeral` variable, that expression implicitly becomes ephemeral.\n\n```hcl\nvariable \"password\" {\n  type      = string\n  ephemeral = true\n}\n\nlocals {\n  # local.database_password is implicitly ephemeral because \n  # var.password is ephemeral.\n  database_password = var.password\n}\n```\n\nThe `local.database_password` value is implicitly ephemeral because it depends on `var.password`.\n\n### Suppressing Values in CLI Output\n\n[inpage-sensitive]: #suppressing-values-in-cli-output\n\n> **Hands-on:** Try the [Protect Sensitive Input Variables](/terraform/tutorials/configuration-language/sensitive-variables?utm_source=WEBSITE&utm_medium=WEB_IO&utm_offer=ARTICLE_PAGE&utm_content=DOCS) tutorial.\n\nSetting a variable as `sensitive` prevents Terraform from showing its value in\nthe `plan` or `apply` output, when you use that variable elsewhere in your\nconfiguration.\n\nTerraform will still record sensitive values in the [state](/terraform/language/state),\nand so anyone who can access the state data will have access to the sensitive\nvalues in cleartext. If you want to omit a value from state, mark that value as [`ephemeral`](#ephemeral). For more information, refer to\n[Sensitive Data in State](/terraform/language/state/sensitive-data).\n\nDeclare a variable as sensitive by setting the `sensitive` argument to `true`:\n\n```hcl\nvariable \"user_information\" {\n  type = object({\n    name    = string\n    address = string\n  })\n  sensitive = true\n}\n\nresource \"some_resource\" \"a\" {\n  name    = var.user_information.name\n  address = var.user_information.address\n}\n```\n\nAny expressions whose result depends on the sensitive variable will be treated\nas sensitive themselves, and so in the above example the two arguments of\n`resource \"some_resource\" \"a\"` will also be hidden in the plan output:\n\n```\nTerraform will perform the following actions:\n\n  # some_resource.a will be created\n  + resource \"some_resource\" \"a\" {\n      + name    = (sensitive value)\n      + address = (sensitive value)\n    }\n\nPlan: 1 to add, 0 to change, 0 to destroy.\n```\n\nIn some cases where you use a sensitive variable inside a nested block, Terraform\nmay treat the entire block as redacted. This happens for resource types where\nall of the blocks of a particular type are required to be unique, and so\ndisclosing the content of one block might imply the content of a sibling block.\n\n```\n  # some_resource.a will be updated in-place\n  ~ resource \"some_resource\" \"a\" {\n      ~ nested_block {\n          # At least one attribute in this block is (or was) sensitive,\n          # so its contents will not be displayed.\n        }\n    }\n```\n\nA provider can also\n[declare an attribute as sensitive](/terraform/plugin/sdkv2/best-practices/sensitive-state#using-the-sensitive-flag),\nwhich will cause Terraform to hide it from regular output regardless of how\nyou assign it a value. For more information, see\n[Sensitive Resource Attributes](/terraform/language/expressions/references#sensitive-resource-attributes).\n\nIf you use a sensitive value as part of an\n[output value](/terraform/language/values/outputs) then Terraform will require\nyou to also mark the output value itself as sensitive, to confirm that you\nintended to export it.\n\n#### Cases where Terraform may disclose a sensitive variable\n\nA `sensitive` variable is a configuration-centered concept, and values are sent to providers without any obfuscation. A provider error could disclose a value if that value is included in the error message. For example, a provider might return the following error even if \"foo\" is a sensitive value: `\"Invalid value 'foo' for field\"`\n\nIf a resource attribute is used as, or part of, the provider-defined resource id, an `apply` will disclose the value. In the example below, the `prefix` attribute has been set to a sensitive variable, but then that value (\"jae\") is later disclosed as part of the resource id:\n\n```\n  # random_pet.animal will be created\n  + resource \"random_pet\" \"animal\" {\n      + id        = (known after apply)\n      + length    = 2\n      + prefix    = (sensitive value)\n      + separator = \"-\"\n    }\n\nPlan: 1 to add, 0 to change, 0 to destroy.\n\n...\n\nrandom_pet.animal: Creating...\nrandom_pet.animal: Creation complete after 0s [id=jae-known-mongoose]\n```\n\n### Disallowing Null Input Values\n\n[inpage-nullable]: #disallowing-null-input-values\n\n-> This feature is available in Terraform v1.1.0 and later.\n\nThe `nullable` argument in a variable block controls whether the module caller\nmay assign the value `null` to the variable.\n\n```hcl\nvariable \"example\" {\n  type     = string\n  nullable = false\n}\n```\n\nThe default value for `nullable` is `true`. When `nullable` is `true`, `null`\nis a valid value for the variable, and the module configuration must always\naccount for the possibility of the variable value being `null`. Passing a\n`null` value as a module input argument will override any `default` value.\n\nSetting `nullable` to `false` ensures that the variable value will never be\n`null` within the module. If `nullable` is `false` and the variable has a\n`default` value, then Terraform uses the default when a module input argument is `null`.\n\nThe `nullable` argument only controls where the direct value of the variable may be `null`.\nFor variables of collection or structural types, such as lists or objects,\nthe caller may still use `null` in nested elements or attributes, as long as\nthe collection or structure itself is not null.\n\n## Using Input Variable Values\n\nWithin the module that declared a variable, its value can be accessed from\nwithin [expressions](/terraform/language/expressions) as `var.<NAME>`,\nwhere `<NAME>` matches the label given in the declaration block:\n\n-> **Note:** Input variables are _created_ by a `variable` block, but you\n_reference_ them as attributes on an object named `var`.\n\n```hcl\nresource \"aws_instance\" \"example\" {\n  instance_type = \"t2.micro\"\n  ami           = var.image_id\n}\n```\n\nThe value assigned to a variable can only be accessed in expressions within\nthe module where it was declared.\n\n## Assigning Values to Root Module Variables\n\nWhen variables are declared in the root module of your configuration, they\ncan be set in a number of ways:\n\n* [In an HCP Terraform workspace](/terraform/cloud-docs/workspaces/variables).\n* Individually, with the `-var` command line option.\n* In variable definitions (`.tfvars`) files, either specified on the command line\n  or automatically loaded.\n* As environment variables.\n\nThe following sections describe these options in more detail. This section does\nnot apply to _child_ modules, where values for input variables are instead\nassigned in the configuration of their parent module, as described in\n[_Modules_](/terraform/language/modules).\n\n### Variables on the Command Line\n\nTo specify individual variables on the command line, use the `-var` option\nwhen running the `terraform plan` and `terraform apply` commands:\n\n```\nterraform apply -var=\"image_id=ami-abc123\"\nterraform apply -var='image_id_list=[\"ami-abc123\",\"ami-def456\"]' -var=\"instance_type=t2.micro\"\nterraform apply -var='image_id_map={\"us-east-1\":\"ami-abc123\",\"us-east-2\":\"ami-def456\"}'\n```\n\nThe above examples show appropriate syntax for Unix-style shells, such as on\nLinux or macOS. For more information on shell quoting, including additional\nexamples for Windows Command Prompt, see\n[Input Variables on the Command Line](/terraform/cli/commands/plan#input-variables-on-the-command-line).\n\nYou can use the `-var` option multiple times in a single command to set several\ndifferent variables.\n\n<a id=\"variable-files\"></a>\n\n### Variable Definitions (`.tfvars`) Files\n\nTo set lots of variables, it is more convenient to specify their values in\na _variable definitions file_ (with a filename ending in either `.tfvars`\nor `.tfvars.json`) and then specify that file on the command line with\n`-var-file`:\n\nLinux, Mac OS, and UNIX:\n\n```shell\nterraform apply -var-file=\"testing.tfvars\"\n```\n\nPowerShell:\n\n```shell\nterraform apply -var-file='testing.tfvars'\n```\n\nWindows `cmd.exe`:\n\n```shell\nterraform apply -var-file=\"testing.tfvars\"\n```\n\n-> **Note:** This is how HCP Terraform passes\n[workspace variables](/terraform/cloud-docs/workspaces/variables) to Terraform.\n\nA variable definitions file uses the same basic syntax as Terraform language\nfiles, but consists only of variable name assignments:\n\n```hcl\nimage_id = \"ami-abc123\"\navailability_zone_names = [\n  \"us-east-1a\",\n  \"us-west-1c\",\n]\n```\n\nTerraform also automatically loads a number of variable definitions files\nif they are present:\n\n* Files named exactly `terraform.tfvars` or `terraform.tfvars.json`.\n* Any files with names ending in `.auto.tfvars` or `.auto.tfvars.json`.\n\nFiles whose names end with `.json` are parsed instead as JSON objects, with\nthe root object properties corresponding to variable names:\n\n```json\n{\n  \"image_id\": \"ami-abc123\",\n  \"availability_zone_names\": [\"us-west-1a\", \"us-west-1c\"]\n}\n```\n\n### Environment Variables\n\nAs a fallback for the other ways of defining variables, Terraform searches\nthe environment of its own process for environment variables named `TF_VAR_`\nfollowed by the name of a declared variable.\n\nThis can be useful when running Terraform in automation, or when running a\nsequence of Terraform commands in succession with the same variables.\nFor example, at a `bash` prompt on a Unix system:\n\n```\n$ export TF_VAR_image_id=ami-abc123\n$ terraform plan\n...\n```\n\nOn operating systems where environment variable names are case-sensitive,\nTerraform matches the variable name exactly as given in configuration, and\nso the required environment variable name will usually have a mix of upper\nand lower case letters as in the above example.\n\n### Complex-typed Values\n\nWhen variable values are provided in a variable definitions file, you can use\nTerraform's usual syntax for\n[literal expressions](/terraform/language/expressions/types#literal-expressions)\nto assign complex-typed values, like lists and maps.\n\nSome special rules apply to the `-var` command line option and to environment\nvariables. For convenience, Terraform defaults to interpreting `-var` and\nenvironment variable values as literal strings, which need only shell quoting,\nand no special quoting for Terraform. For example, in a Unix-style shell:\n\n```\n$ export TF_VAR_image_id='ami-abc123'\n```\n\nHowever, if a root module variable uses a [type constraint](#type-constraints)\nto require a complex value (list, set, map, object, or tuple), Terraform will\ninstead attempt to parse its value using the same syntax used within variable\ndefinitions files, which requires careful attention to the string escaping rules\nin your shell:\n\n```\n$ export TF_VAR_availability_zone_names='[\"us-west-1b\",\"us-west-1d\"]'\n```\n\nFor readability, and to avoid the need to worry about shell escaping, we\nrecommend always setting complex variable values via variable definitions files.\nFor more information on quoting and escaping for `-var` arguments,\nsee\n[Input Variables on the Command Line](/terraform/cli/commands/plan#input-variables-on-the-command-line).\n\n### Values for Undeclared Variables\n\nIf you have defined a variable value, but not its corresponding `variable {}`\ndefinition, you may get an error or warning depending on how you have provided\nthat value.\n\nIf you provide values for undeclared variables defined as [environment variables](#environment-variables)\nyou will not get an error or warning. This is because environment variables may\nbe declared but not used in all configurations that might be run.\n\nIf you provide values for undeclared variables defined [in a file](#variable-definitions-tfvars-files)\nyou will get a warning. This is to help in cases where you have provided a variable\nvalue _meant_ for a variable declaration, but perhaps there is a mistake in the\nvalue definition. For example, the following configuration:\n\n```terraform\nvariable \"moose\" {\n  type = string\n}\n```\n\nAnd the following `.tfvars` file:\n\n```hcl\nmosse = \"Moose\"\n```\n\nWill cause Terraform to warn you that there is no variable declared `\"mosse\"`, which can help\nyou spot this mistake.\n\nIf you use `.tfvars` files across multiple configurations and expect to continue to see this warning,\nyou can use the [`-compact-warnings`](/terraform/cli/commands/plan#compact-warnings)\noption to simplify your output.\n\nIf you provide values for undeclared variables on the [command line](#variables-on-the-command-line),\nTerraform will return an error. To avoid this error, either declare a variable block for the value, or remove\nthe variable value from your Terraform call.\n\n### Variable Definition Precedence\n\nThe above mechanisms for setting variables can be used together in any\ncombination. If the same variable is assigned multiple values, Terraform uses\nthe _last_ value it finds, overriding any previous values. Note that the same\nvariable cannot be assigned multiple values within a single source.\n\nTerraform loads variables in the following order, with later sources taking\nprecedence over earlier ones:\n\n* Environment variables\n* The `terraform.tfvars` file, if present.\n* The `terraform.tfvars.json` file, if present.\n* Any `*.auto.tfvars` or `*.auto.tfvars.json` files, processed in lexical order\n  of their filenames.\n* Any `-var` and `-var-file` options on the command line, in the order they\n  are provided. (This includes variables set by an HCP Terraform\n  workspace.)\n\n~> **Important:** In Terraform 0.12 and later, variables with map and object\nvalues behave the same way as other variables: the last value found overrides\nthe previous values. This is a change from previous versions of Terraform, which\nwould _merge_ map values instead of overriding them.\n\n#### Variable precedence within Terraform tests\n\nWithin Terraform test files, you can specify variable values within\n`variables` blocks, either nested within `run` blocks or defined directly within\nthe file.\n\nVariables defined in this way take precedence over all other mechanisms during\ntest execution, with variables defined within `run` blocks taking precedence\nover those defined within the file.\n","numLines":583,"startLine":1,"totalLines":583}}}

The workflows of ephemeral resources are subject to change.\n\n## Introduction\n\nEphemeral resources are Terraform resources that are essentially temporary. Ephemeral resources have a unique lifecycle, and Terraform does not store them in its state. Each `ephemeral` block describes one or more ephemeral resources, such as a temporary password or connection to another system.\n\n<!-- After page exists, add: For information about how Terraform manages ephemeral resources, refer to\n[Epehemeral Values](/terraform/language/epehemeral-values). -->\n\nAn `ephemeral` block declares an ephemeral resource of a specific type with a\nspecific local name, much like a `resource` block. Terraform uses an ephemeral resource's name to\nrefer to that resource in the same module, but an ephemeral resource's name has no meaning\noutside that module's scope.\n\n\n## Lifecycle\n\nThe lifecycle of an ephemeral resource is different from resources and data sources. When Terraform provisions ephemeral resources, it performs the following steps:\n\n1. If Terraform needs to access the result of an ephemeral resource, it opens\nthat ephemeral resource. For example, if Terraform opens an ephemeral resource for a Vault secret, the Vault provider obtains a lease and returns a secret. \n\n1. If Terraform needs access to the ephemeral resource for longer than the\nremote system's enforced expiration time, Terraform asks the provider\nto renew it periodically. For example, if Terraform renews a Vault secret ephemeral resource, the Vault provider then calls Vault's lease renewal API endpoint to extend the expiration time.\n\n1. Once Terraform no longer needs an ephemeral resource, Terraform closes\nit. This happens after the providers that depend on a certain ephemeral resource\ncomplete all of their work for the current Terraform run phase. For example, closing a Vault secret ephemeral resource means the Vault provider explicitly ends the lease, allowing Vault to immediately revoke the associated credentials.\n\nTerraform follows these lifecycle steps for each instance of an ephemeral\nresource in a given configuration.\n\n## Dependency graph\n\nEphemeral resources form nodes in Terraform's dependency graph, which interact similarly as resources and data sources. For example, when a resource or data source depends on an attribute of an ephemeral resource, Terraform automatically provisions the ephemeral resource first.\n\n## Configuration model\n\nMost of the arguments within the body of an `ephemeral` block are specific to the ephemeral resource you are defining. {"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"tool_use_id":"toolu_01MbJgtxLTmwTmrWBEvBvoum","type":"tool_result","content":"     1→---\n     2→page_title: Output Values - Configuration Language\n     3→description: Output values are the return values of a Terraform module.\n     4→---\n     5→\n     6→# Output Values\n     7→\n     8→Output values make information about your infrastructure available on the\n     9→command line, and can expose information for other Terraform configurations to\n    10→use. Output values are similar to return values in programming languages.\n    11→\n    12→> **Hands-on:** Try the [Output Data From\n    13→> Terraform](/terraform/tutorials/configuration-language/outputs)\n    14→> tutorial.\n    15→\n    16→Output values have several uses:\n    17→\n    18→- A child module can use outputs to expose a subset of its resource attributes\n    19→  to a parent module.\n    20→- A root module can use outputs to print certain values in the CLI output after\n    21→  running `terraform apply`.\n    22→- When using [remote state](/terraform/language/state/remote), root module outputs can be\n    23→  accessed by other configurations via a\n    24→  [`terraform_remote_state` data source](/terraform/language/state/remote-state-data).\n    25→\n    26→Resource instances managed by Terraform each export attributes whose values\n    27→can be used elsewhere in configuration. Output values are a way to expose some\n    28→of that information to the user of your module.\n    29→\n    30→-> **Note:** For brevity, output values are often referred to as just \"outputs\"\n    31→when the meaning is clear from context.\n    32→\n    33→## Declaring an Output Value\n    34→\n    35→Each output value exported by a module must be declared using an `output`\n    36→block:\n    37→\n    38→```hcl\n    39→output \"instance_ip_addr\" {\n    40→  value = aws_instance.server.private_ip\n    41→}\n    42→```\n    43→\n    44→The label immediately after the `output` keyword is the name, which must be a\n    45→valid [identifier](/terraform/language/syntax/configuration#identifiers). In a root module, this name is\n    46→displayed to the user; in a child module, it can be used to access the output's\n    47→value.\n    48→\n    49→The `value` argument takes an [expression](/terraform/language/expressions)\n    50→whose result is to be returned to the user. In this example, the expression\n    51→refers to the `private_ip` attribute exposed by an `aws_instance` resource\n    52→defined elsewhere in this module (not shown). Any valid expression is allowed\n    53→as an output value.\n    54→\n    55→-> **Note:** Outputs are only rendered when Terraform applies your plan. Running\n    56→`terraform plan` will not render outputs.\n    57→\n    58→## Accessing Child Module Outputs\n    59→\n    60→In a parent module, outputs of child modules are available in expressions as\n    61→`module.<MODULE NAME>.<OUTPUT NAME>`. For example, if a child module named\n    62→`web_server` declared an output named `instance_ip_addr`, you could access that\n    63→value as `module.web_server.instance_ip_addr`.\n    64→\n    65→\n    66→## Custom Condition Checks\n    67→\n    68→You can use `precondition` blocks to specify guarantees about output data. The following examples creates a precondition that checks whether the EC2 instance has an encrypted root volume.\n    69→\n    70→```hcl\n    71→output \"api_base_url\" {\n    72→  value = \"https://${aws_instance.example.private_dns}:8433/\"\n    73→\n    74→  # The EC2 instance must have an encrypted root volume.\n    75→  precondition {\n    76→    condition     = data.aws_ebs_volume.example.encrypted\n    77→    error_message = \"The server's root volume is not encrypted.\"\n    78→  }\n    79→}\n    80→```\n    81→\n    82→Custom conditions can help capture assumptions, helping future maintainers understand the configuration design and intent. They also return useful information about errors earlier and in context, helping consumers more easily diagnose issues in their configurations.\n    83→\n    84→Refer to [Custom Condition Checks](/terraform/language/expressions/custom-conditions#preconditions-and-postconditions) for more details.\n    85→\n    86→## Optional Arguments\n    87→\n    88→`output` blocks can optionally include `description`, `sensitive`, `ephemeral`, and `depends_on` arguments, which are described in the following sections.\n    89→\n    90→<a id=\"description\"></a>\n    91→\n    92→### `description` — Output Value Documentation\n    93→\n    94→Because the output values of a module are part of its user interface, you can\n    95→briefly describe the purpose of each value using the optional `description`\n    96→argument:\n    97→\n    98→```hcl\n    99→output \"instance_ip_addr\" {\n   100→  value       = aws_instance.server.private_ip\n   101→  description = \"The private IP address of the main server instance.\"\n   102→}\n   103→```\n   104→\n   105→The description should concisely explain the\n   106→purpose of the output and what kind of value is expected. This description\n   107→string might be included in documentation about the module, and so it should be\n   108→written from the perspective of the user of the module rather than its\n   109→maintainer. For commentary for module maintainers, use comments.\n   110→\n   111→<a id=\"ephemeral\"></a>\n   112→\n   113→### `ephemeral` — Avoid storing values in state or plan files\n   114→\n   115→-> **Note**: Ephemeral outputs are in public beta and available in Terraform v1.10 and later. The workflows of ephemeral outputs are subject to change.\n   116→\n   117→You can mark `output` values as `ephemeral` in child modules to pass ephemeral values between modules while avoiding persisting those values to state or plan files. This is useful for managing credentials, tokens, or other temporary resources you do not want to store in Terraform state files.\n   118→\n   119→You can mark an `output` in a child module as ephemeral by setting the `ephemeral` attribute to `true`:\n   120→\n   121→```hcl\n   122→# modules/db/main.tf\n   123→\n   124→output \"secret_id\" {\n   125→  value       = aws_secretsmanager_secret.secret_id\n   126→  description = \"Temporary secret ID for accessing database in AWS.\"\n   127→  ephemeral   = true\n   128→}\n   129→```\n   130→\n   131→Terraform has access to the value of `output` blocks during plan and apply operations. At the end of a plan or apply operation, Terraform does not persist the value of any ephemeral outputs.\n   132→\n   133→You can only reference ephemeral outputs in specific contexts or Terraform throws an error. The following are valid contexts for referencing ephemeral outputs:\n   134→* In another child module's ephemeral `output` block\n   135→* In [ephemeral variables](/terraform/language/values/variables#exclude-values-from-state)\n   136→* In [ephemeral resources](/terraform/language/resources/ephemeral)\n   137→\n   138→Note that you cannot set an `output` value as `ephemeral` in the root module.\n   139→\n   140→\n   141→<a id=\"sensitive\"></a>\n   142→\n   143→### `sensitive` — Suppressing Values in CLI Output\n   144→\n   145→An output can be marked as containing sensitive material using the optional\n   146→`sensitive` argument:\n   147→\n   148→```hcl\n   149→output \"db_password\" {\n   150→  value       = aws_db_instance.db.password\n   151→  description = \"The password for logging in to the database.\"\n   152→  sensitive   = true\n   153→}\n   154→```\n   155→\n   156→Terraform will hide values marked as sensitive in the messages from\n   157→`terraform plan` and `terraform apply`. In the following scenario, our root\n   158→module has an output declared as sensitive and a module call with a\n   159→sensitive output, which we then use in a resource attribute.\n   160→\n   161→```hcl\n   162→# main.tf\n   163→\n   164→module \"foo\" {\n   165→  source = \"./mod\"\n   166→}\n   167→\n   168→resource \"test_instance\" \"x\" {\n   169→  some_attribute = module.foo.a # resource attribute references a sensitive output\n   170→}\n   171→\n   172→output \"out\" {\n   173→  value     = \"xyz\"\n   174→  sensitive = true\n   175→}\n   176→\n   177→# mod/main.tf, our module containing a sensitive output\n   178→\n   179→output \"a\" {\n   180→  value     = \"secret\"\n   181→  sensitive = true\n   182→}\n   183→```\n   184→\n   185→When we run a plan or apply, the sensitive value is redacted from output:\n   186→\n   187→```\n   188→Terraform will perform the following actions:\n   189→\n   190→  # test_instance.x will be created\n   191→  + resource \"test_instance\" \"x\" {\n   192→      + some_attribute    = (sensitive value)\n   193→    }\n   194→\n   195→Plan: 1 to add, 0 to change, 0 to destroy.\n   196→\n   197→Changes to Outputs:\n   198→  + out = (sensitive value)\n   199→```\n   200→\n   201→Terraform will still record sensitive values in the [state](/terraform/language/state),\n   202→and so anyone who can access the state data will have access to the sensitive\n   203→values in cleartext. For more information, see\n   204→[Sensitive Data in State](/terraform/language/state/sensitive-data).\n   205→\n   206→<a id=\"depends_on\"></a>\n   207→\n   208→### `depends_on` — Explicit Output Dependencies\n   209→\n   210→Since output values are just a means for passing data out of a module, it is\n   211→usually not necessary to worry about their relationships with other nodes in\n   212→the dependency graph.\n   213→\n   214→However, when a parent module accesses an output value exported by one of its\n   215→child modules, the dependencies of that output value allow Terraform to\n   216→correctly determine the dependencies between resources defined in different\n   217→modules.\n   218→\n   219→Just as with\n   220→[resource dependencies](/terraform/language/resources/behavior#resource-dependencies),\n   221→Terraform analyzes the `value` expression for an output value and automatically\n   222→determines a set of dependencies, but in less-common cases there are\n   223→dependencies that cannot be recognized implicitly. In these rare cases, the\n   224→`depends_on` argument can be used to create additional explicit dependencies:\n   225→\n   226→```hcl\n   227→output \"instance_ip_addr\" {\n   228→  value       = aws_instance.server.private_ip\n   229→  description = \"The private IP address of the main server instance.\"\n   230→\n   231→  depends_on = [\n   232→    # Security group rule must be created before this IP address could\n   233→    # actually be used, otherwise the services will be unreachable.\n   234→    aws_security_group_rule.local_access,\n   235→  ]\n   236→}\n   237→```\n   238→\n   239→The `depends_on` argument should be used only as a last resort.
