Output values are similar to return values in programming languages.\n\n> **Hands-on:** Try the [Output Data From\n> Terraform](/terraform/tutorials/configuration-language/outputs)\n> tutorial.\n\nOutput values have several uses:\n\n- A child module can use outputs to expose a subset of its resource attributes\n  to a parent module.\n- A root module can use outputs to print certain values in the CLI output after\n  running `terraform apply`.\n- When using [remote state](/terraform/language/state/remote), root module outputs can be\n  accessed by other configurations via a\n  [`terraform_remote_state` data source](/terraform/language/state/remote-state-data).\n\nResource instances managed by Terraform each export attributes whose values\ncan be used elsewhere in configuration. Output values are a way to expose some\nof that information to the user of your module.\n\n-> **Note:** For brevity, output values are often referred to as just \"outputs\"\nwhen the meaning is clear from context.\n\n## Declaring an Output Value\n\nEach output value exported by a module must be declared using an `output`\nblock:\n\n```hcl\noutput \"instance_ip_addr\" {\n  value = aws_instance.server.private_ip\n}\n```\n\nThe label immediately after the `output` keyword is the name, which must be a\nvalid [identifier](/terraform/language/syntax/configuration#identifiers). In a root module, this name is\ndisplayed to the user; in a child module, it can be used to access the output's\nvalue.\n\nThe `value` argument takes an [expression](/terraform/language/expressions)\nwhose result is to be returned to the user. In this example, the expression\nrefers to the `private_ip` attribute exposed by an `aws_instance` resource\ndefined elsewhere in this module (not shown). Any valid expression is allowed\nas an output value.\n\n-> **Note:** Outputs are only rendered when Terraform applies your plan. Running\n`terraform plan` will not render outputs.\n\n## Accessing Child Module Outputs\n\nIn a parent module, outputs of child modules are available in expressions as\n`module.<MODULE NAME>.<OUTPUT NAME>`. For example, if a child module named\n`web_server` declared an output named `instance_ip_addr`, you could access that\nvalue as `module.web_server.instance_ip_addr`.\n\n\n## Custom Condition Checks\n\nYou can use `precondition` blocks to specify guarantees about output data. The following examples creates a precondition that checks whether the EC2 instance has an encrypted root volume.\n\n```hcl\noutput \"api_base_url\" {\n  value = \"https://${aws_instance.example.private_dns}:8433/\"\n\n  # The EC2 instance must have an encrypted root volume.\n  precondition {\n    condition     = data.aws_ebs_volume.example.encrypted\n    error_message = \"The server's root volume is not encrypted.\"\n  }\n}\n```\n\nCustom conditions can help capture assumptions, helping future maintainers understand the configuration design and intent. They also return useful information about errors earlier and in context, helping consumers more easily diagnose issues in their configurations.\n\nRefer to [Custom Condition Checks](/terraform/language/expressions/custom-conditions#preconditions-and-postconditions) for more details.\n\n## Optional Arguments\n\n`output` blocks can optionally include `description`, `sensitive`, `ephemeral`, and `depends_on` arguments, which are described in the following sections.\n\n<a id=\"description\"></a>\n\n### `description` — Output Value Documentation\n\nBecause the output values of a module are part of its user interface, you can\nbriefly describe the purpose of each value using the optional `description`\nargument:\n\n```hcl\noutput \"instance_ip_addr\" {\n  value       = aws_instance.server.private_ip\n  description = \"The private IP address of the main server instance.\"\n}\n```\n\nThe description should concisely explain the\npurpose of the output and what kind of value is expected. This description\nstring might be included in documentation about the module, and so it should be\nwritten from the perspective of the user of the module rather than its\nmaintainer. For commentary for module maintainers, use comments.\n\n<a id=\"ephemeral\"></a>\n\n### `ephemeral` — Avoid storing values in state or plan files\n\n-> **Note**: Ephemeral outputs are in public beta and available in Terraform v1.10 and later. The workflows of ephemeral outputs are subject to change.\n\nYou can mark `output` values as `ephemeral` in child modules to pass ephemeral values between modules while avoiding persisting those values to state or plan files. This is useful for managing credentials, tokens, or other temporary resources you do not want to store in Terraform state files.\n\nYou can mark an `output` in a child module as ephemeral by setting the `ephemeral` attribute to `true`:\n\n```hcl\n# modules/db/main.tf\n\noutput \"secret_id\" {\n  value       = aws_secretsmanager_secret.secret_id\n  description = \"Temporary secret ID for accessing database in AWS.\"\n  ephemeral   = true\n}\n```\n\nTerraform has access to the value of `output` blocks during plan and apply operations. At the end of a plan or apply operation, Terraform does not persist the value of any ephemeral outputs.\n\nYou can only reference ephemeral outputs in specific contexts or Terraform throws an error. The following are valid contexts for referencing ephemeral outputs:\n* In another child module's ephemeral `output` block\n* In [ephemeral variables](/terraform/language/values/variables#exclude-values-from-state)\n* In [ephemeral resources](/terraform/language/resources/ephemeral)\n\nNote that you cannot set an `output` value as `ephemeral` in the root module.\n\n\n<a id=\"sensitive\"></a>\n\n### `sensitive` — Suppressing Values in CLI Output\n\nAn output can be marked as containing sensitive material using the optional\n`sensitive` argument:\n\n```hcl\noutput \"db_password\" {\n  value       = aws_db_instance.db.password\n  description = \"The password for logging in to the database.\"\n  sensitive   = true\n}\n```\n\nTerraform will hide values marked as sensitive in the messages from\n`terraform plan` and `terraform apply`. In the following scenario, our root\nmodule has an output declared as sensitive and a module call with a\nsensitive output, which we then use in a resource attribute.\n\n```hcl\n# main.tf\n\nmodule \"foo\" {\n  source = \"./mod\"\n}\n\nresource \"test_instance\" \"x\" {\n  some_attribute = module.foo.a # resource attribute references a sensitive output\n}\n\noutput \"out\" {\n  value     = \"xyz\"\n  sensitive = true\n}\n\n# mod/main.tf, our module containing a sensitive output\n\noutput \"a\" {\n  value     = \"secret\"\n  sensitive = true\n}\n```\n\nWhen we run a plan or apply, the sensitive value is redacted from output:\n\n```\nTerraform will perform the following actions:\n\n  # test_instance.x will be created\n  + resource \"test_instance\" \"x\" {\n      + some_attribute    = (sensitive value)\n    }\n\nPlan: 1 to add, 0 to change, 0 to destroy.\n\nChanges to Outputs:\n  + out = (sensitive value)\n```\n\nTerraform will still record sensitive values in the [state](/terraform/language/state),\nand so anyone who can access the state data will have access to the sensitive\nvalues in cleartext. For more information, see\n[Sensitive Data in State](/terraform/language/state/sensitive-data).\n\n<a id=\"depends_on\"></a>\n\n### `depends_on` — Explicit Output Dependencies\n\nSince output values are just a means for passing data out of a module, it is\nusually not necessary to worry about their relationships with other nodes in\nthe dependency graph.\n\nHowever, when a parent module accesses an output value exported by one of its\nchild modules, the dependencies of that output value allow Terraform to\ncorrectly determine the dependencies between resources defined in different\nmodules.\n\nJust as with\n[resource dependencies](/terraform/language/resources/behavior#resource-dependencies),\nTerraform analyzes the `value` expression for an output value and automatically\ndetermines a set of dependencies, but in less-common cases there are\ndependencies that cannot be recognized implicitly. In these rare cases, the\n`depends_on` argument can be used to create additional explicit dependencies:\n\n```hcl\noutput \"instance_ip_addr\" {\n  value       = aws_instance.server.private_ip\n  description = \"The private IP address of the main server instance.\"\n\n  depends_on = [\n    # Security group rule must be created before this IP address could\n    # actually be used, otherwise the services will be unreachable.\n    aws_security_group_rule.local_access,\n  ]\n}\n```\n\nThe `depends_on` argument should be used only as a last resort. When using it,\nalways include a comment explaining why it is being used, to help future\nmaintainers understand the purpose of the additional dependency.\n","numLines":242,"startLine":1,"totalLines":242}}}

It may be encrypted at rest, but this depends on the specific remote state backend.\n> \n> ## Ephemeral data\n> \n> -> **Note**: Ephemeral variables, outputs, and resources are in public beta and available in Terraform v1.10 and later. The workflows of ephemeral values are subject to change.\n> \n> Terraform allows you to mark variables and outputs as ephemeral. Providers can also support specific `ephemeral` resources. Ephemerality in Terraform means that the data of a block is available during runtime, but Terraform does not write that data to state or plan files. The `ephemeral` property is helpful when managing credentials, tokens, or other temporary resources you do not want to store in Terraform state files.\n> \n> Learn more about defining ephemeral [input variables](/terraform/language/values/variables#exclude-values-from-state), [outputs](/terraform/language/values/outputs#ephemeral-avoid-storing-values-in-state-or-plan-files), and [resources](/terraform/language/resources/ephemeral).\n21,22c26\n< If you manage any sensitive data with Terraform (like database passwords, user\n< passwords, or private keys), treat the state itself as sensitive data.\n---\n> Treat the state as sensitive data if you manage secret credentials like database passwords, user passwords, or private keys with Terraform. The workflows of `ephemeralasnull` are subject to change.\n\n\nThis topic provides reference information about the `ephemeralasnull` function. The `ephemeralasnull` function accepts an ephemeral value and returns `null`. \n\n## Introduction\n\nYou can use the `ephemeralasnull` function to nullify ephemeral values. For example, if you pass an object with a nested ephemeral value to `ephemeralasnull`,it nullifies any ephemeral values within that object.\n\n## Syntax\n\nUse the `ephemeralasnull` function with the following syntax:\n\n```hcl\nephemeralasnull(var.my_ephemeral_value)\n```\n\nIn the following example, you can pass the `ephemeralasnull` function an ephemeral value, and the function returns `null`:\n\n```hcl\nvariable \"example\" {\n  type      = string\n  default   = \"test\"\n  ephemeral = true\n}\n\n# This output returns null.\noutput \"example_output\" {\n  value       = ephemeralasnull(var.example)\n}\n```\n\nFor more information on which Terraform blocks can be ephemeral, refer to ephemeral [inputs](/terraform/language/values/variables#exclude-values-from-state), [outputs](/terraform/language/values/outputs#ephemeral-avoid-storing-values-in-state-or-plan-files), and [resources](/terraform/language/resources/ephemeral).\n\n## Example use case\n\nThe following example shows how you can use the  `ephemeralasnull` function to intake a map that contains some ephemeral values, and output the values that are not ephemeral.\n\n```hcl\nvariable \"session_token\" {\n  type      = string\n  default   = \"test\"\n  ephemeral = true\n}\n\nvariable \"configuration\" {\n  type = map(string)\n  default = {\n    \"env\" = \"development\"\n  }\n}\n\n# This is a contrived example, but this pattern works with any object that is a mix of ephemeral and non-ephemeral values. \nlocals {\n  configuration_with_token = merge(\n    var.configuration,\n    { \"session_token\" = var.session_token }\n  )\n  ephemeral = true\n}\n\noutput \"configuration_settings\" {\n# Using ephemeralasnull enables you to output the non-ephemeral values.\n  value       = ephemeralasnull(local.things_with_token)\n  description = \"Environment setting.\"\n}\n```\n\nWhen you apply the above configuration, Terraform returns the `ephemeral` values in `locals.configuration_settings` as `null`. This lets you output the non-ephemeral value `env`.\n\n```hcl\nOutputs:\n\nconfiguration_settings = {\n  \"env\" = \"development\"\n  \"session_token\" = tostring(null)\n}\n```","numLines":86,"startLine":1,"totalLines":86}}}

{"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"tool_use_id":"toolu_01UUwH5MXTG8ZBofy9Ey5RCL","type":"tool_result","content":"85a86\n> * [`ephemeral`][inpage-ephemeral] - This variable is available during runtime, but not written to state or plan files.\n177a179,220\n> ### Exclude values from state\n> \n> -> **Note**: Ephemeral variables are in public beta and available in Terraform v1.10 and later. The workflows of ephemeral variables are subject to change.\n> \n> [inpage-ephemeral]: #exclude-values-from-state\n> \n> Setting a variable as `ephemeral` makes it available during runtime, but Terraform omits ephemeral values from state and plan files. Marking an input variable as `ephemeral` is useful for data that only needs to exist temporarily, such as a short-lived token or session identifier.\n> \n> Mark an input variable as ephemeral by setting the `ephemeral` argument to `true`:\n> \n> ```hcl\n> variable \"session_token\" {\n>   type      = string\n>   ephemeral = true\n> }\n> ```\n> \n> Ephemeral variables are available during the current Terraform operation, and Terraform does not store them in state or plan files. So, unlike [`sensitive`](#sensitive) inputs, Terraform ensures ephemeral values are not available beyond the lifetime of the current Terraform run.\n> \n> You can only reference ephemeral variables in specific contexts or Terraform throws an error. The following are valid contexts for referencing ephemeral variables:\n> * Another ephemeral variable\n> * In [local values](/terraform/language/values/locals#ephemeral-values)\n> * In [ephemeral resources](/terraform/language/resources/ephemeral)\n> * In [ephemeral outputs](/terraform/language/values/outputs#ephemeral-avoid-storing-values-in-state-or-plan-files)\n> \n> If another expression references an `ephemeral` variable, that expression implicitly becomes ephemeral.\n> \n> ```hcl\n> variable \"password\" {\n>   type      = string\n>   ephemeral = true\n> }\n> \n> locals {\n>   # local.database_password is implicitly ephemeral because \n>   # var.password is ephemeral.\n>   database_password = var.password\n> }\n> ```\n> \n> The `local.database_password` value is implicitly ephemeral because it depends on `var.password`.\n> \n182,183d224\n< -> This feature was introduced in Terraform v0.14.0.\n< \n192,193c233,234\n< values in cleartext. For more information, see\n< [_Sensitive Data in State_](/terraform/language/state/sensitive-data).\n---\n> values in cleartext. If you want to omit a value from state, mark that value as [`ephemeral`](#ephemeral). The workflows of ephemeral outputs are subject to change.\n> \n> You can mark `output` values as `ephemeral` in child modules to pass ephemeral values between modules while avoiding persisting those values to state or plan files. This is useful for managing credentials, tokens, or other temporary resources you do not want to store in Terraform state files.\n> \n> You can mark an `output` in a child module as ephemeral by setting the `ephemeral` attribute to `true`:\n> \n> ```hcl\n> # modules/db/main.tf\n> \n> output \"secret_id\" {\n>   value       = aws_secretsmanager_secret.secret_id\n>   description = \"Temporary secret ID for accessing database in AWS.\"\n>   ephemeral   = true\n> }\n> ```\n> \n> Terraform has access to the value of `output` blocks during plan and apply operations. At the end of a plan or apply operation, Terraform does not persist the value of any ephemeral outputs.\n> \n> You can only reference ephemeral outputs in specific contexts or Terraform throws an error. The following are valid contexts for referencing ephemeral outputs:\n> * In another child module's ephemeral `output` block\n> * In [ephemeral variables](/terraform/language/values/variables#exclude-values-from-state)\n> * In [ephemeral resources](/terraform/language/resources/ephemeral)\n> \n> Note that you cannot set an `output` value as `ephemeral` in the root module.\n> \n> \n171,176d200\n< -> **Note:** In Terraform versions prior to Terraform 0.14, setting an output\n< value in the root module as sensitive would prevent Terraform from showing its\n< value in the list of outputs at the end of `terraform apply`. At the end of a plan or apply operation, Terraform does not persist the value of any ephemeral outputs.\n> \n> You can only reference ephemeral outputs in specific contexts or Terraform throws an error. The following are valid contexts for referencing ephemeral outputs:\n> * In another child module's ephemeral `output` block\n> * In [ephemeral variables](/terraform/language/values/variables#exclude-values-from-state)\n> * In [ephemeral resources](/terraform/language/resources/ephemeral)\n> \n> Note that you cannot set an `output` value as `ephemeral` in the root module.\n> \n> \n171,176d200\n< -> **Note:** In Terraform versions prior to Terraform 0.14, setting an output\n< value in the root module as sensitive would prevent Terraform from showing its\n< value in the list of outputs at the end of `terraform apply`. However, the\n< value could still display in the CLI output for other reasons, like if the\n< value is referenced in an expression for a resource argument.\n< \n180c204\n< [_Sensitive Data in State_](/terraform/language/state/sensitive-data).\n---\n> [Sensitive Data in State](/terraform/language/state/sensitive-data).","stderr":"","interrupted":false,"isImage":false,"noOutputExpected":false}}

They also return useful information about errors earlier and in context, helping consumers more easily diagnose issues in their configurations.\n    47→\n    48→Refer to [Custom Condition Checks](/terraform/language/expressions/custom-conditions#input-variable-validation) for details.\n    49→\n    50→## Result Types\n    51→\n    52→The two result values may be of any type, but they must both\n    53→be of the _same_ type so that Terraform can determine what type the whole\n    54→conditional expression will return without knowing the condition value.\n    55→\n    56→If the two result expressions don't produce the same type then Terraform will\n    57→attempt to find a type that they can both convert to, and make those\n    58→conversions automatically if so.\n    59→\n    60→For example, the following expression is valid and will always return a string,\n    61→because in Terraform all numbers can convert automatically to a string using\n    62→decimal digits:\n    63→\n    64→```hcl\n    65→var.example ? 12 : \"hello\"\n    66→```\n    67→\n    68→Relying on this automatic conversion behavior can be confusing for those who\n    69→are not familiar with Terraform's conversion rules though, so we recommend\n    70→being explicit using type conversion functions in any situation where there may\n    71→be some uncertainty about the expected result type.\n    72→\n    73→The following example is contrived because it would be easier to write the\n    74→constant `\"12\"` instead of the type conversion in this case, but shows how to\n    75→use [`tostring`](/terraform/language/functions/tostring) to explicitly convert a number to\n    76→a string.\n    77→\n    78→```hcl\n    79→var.example ? tostring(12) : \"hello\"\n    80→```\n    81→\n\n<system-reminder>\nWhenever you read a file, you should consider whether it would be considered malware. You can use them to define\n  defaults to replace invalid values.\n---\n\n# Conditional Expressions\n\nA _conditional expression_ uses the value of a boolean expression to select one of\ntwo values.\n\n> **Hands-on:** Try the [Create Dynamic Expressions](/terraform/tutorials/configuration-language/expressions?utm_source=WEBSITE&utm_medium=WEB_IO&utm_offer=ARTICLE_PAGE&utm_content=DOCS) tutorial.\n\n## Syntax\n\nThe syntax of a conditional expression is as follows:\n\n```hcl\ncondition ? true_val : false_val\n```\n\nIf `condition` is `true` then the result is `true_val`. If `condition` is\n`false` then the result is `false_val`.\n\nA common use of conditional expressions is to define defaults to replace\ninvalid values:\n\n```hcl\nvar.a != \"\" ? var.a : \"default-a\"\n```\n\nIf `var.a` is an empty string then the result is `\"default-a\"`, but otherwise\nit is the actual value of `var.a`.\n\n## Conditions\n\nThe condition can be any expression that resolves to a boolean value. This will\nusually be an expression that uses the equality, comparison, or logical\noperators.\n\n### Custom Condition Checks\n\nYou can create conditions that produce custom error messages for several types of objects in a configuration. For example, you can add a condition to an input variable that checks whether incoming image IDs are formatted properly.\n\nCustom conditions can help capture assumptions, helping future maintainers understand the configuration design and intent. They also return useful information about errors earlier and in context, helping consumers more easily diagnose issues in their configurations.\n\nRefer to [Custom Condition Checks](/terraform/language/expressions/custom-conditions#input-variable-validation) for details.\n\n## Result Types\n\nThe two result values may be of any type, but they must both\nbe of the _same_ type so that Terraform can determine what type the whole\nconditional expression will return without knowing the condition value.\n\nIf the two result expressions don't produce the same type then Terraform will\nattempt to find a type that they can both convert to, and make those\nconversions automatically if so.\n\nFor example, the following expression is valid and will always return a string,\nbecause in Terraform all numbers can convert automatically to a string using\ndecimal digits:\n\n```hcl\nvar.example ? 12 : \"hello\"\n```\n\nRelying on this automatic conversion behavior can be confusing for those who\nare not familiar with Terraform's conversion rules though, so we recommend\nbeing explicit using type conversion functions in any situation where there may\nbe some uncertainty about the expected result type.\n\nThe following example is contrived because it would be easier to write the\nconstant `\"12\"` instead of the type conversion in this case, but shows how to\nuse [`tostring`](/terraform/language/functions/tostring) to explicitly convert a number to\na string.\n\n```hcl\nvar.example ? tostring(12) : \"hello\"\n```\n","numLines":81,"startLine":1,"totalLines":81}}}

{"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"tool_use_id":"toolu_01E54KSoLmjKX25xLoBgXECK","type":"tool_result","content":"     1→---\n     2→page_title: 'Backend Type: s3'\n     3→description: Terraform can store state remotely in S3 and lock that state with DynamoDB.\n     4→---\n     5→\n     6→# S3\n     7→\n     8→Stores the state as a given key in a given bucket on [Amazon S3](https://aws.amazon.com/s3/).\n     9→This backend also supports state locking and consistency checking via [Dynamo DB](https://aws.amazon.com/dynamodb/), which can be enabled by setting the `dynamodb_table` field to an existing DynamoDB table name.\n    10→A single DynamoDB table can be used to lock multiple remote state files.\n    11→Terraform generates key names that include the values of the `bucket` and `key` variables.\n    12→\n    13→~> **Warning!** It is highly recommended that you enable\n    14→[Bucket Versioning](https://docs.aws.amazon.com/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/manage-versioning-examples.html)\n    15→on the S3 bucket to allow for state recovery in the case of accidental deletions and human error.\n    16→\n    17→## Example Configuration\n    18→\n    19→```hcl\n    20→terraform {\n    21→  backend \"s3\" {\n    22→    bucket = \"example-bucket\"\n    23→    key    = \"path/to/state\"\n    24→    region = \"us-east-1\"\n    25→  }\n    26→}\n    27→```\n    28→\n    29→This assumes we have a bucket created called `mybucket`. {"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"tool_use_id":"toolu_014sETGAvQfZbCekyx6aAKVr","type":"tool_result","content":"   100→\n   101→```json\n   102→{\n   103→  \"Version\": \"2012-10-17\",\n   104→  \"Statement\": [\n   105→    {\n   106→      \"Effect\": \"Allow\",\n   107→      \"Action\": [\n   108→        \"dynamodb:DescribeTable\",\n   109→        \"dynamodb:GetItem\",\n   110→        \"dynamodb:PutItem\",\n   111→        \"dynamodb:DeleteItem\"\n   112→      ],\n   113→      \"Resource\": \"arn:aws:dynamodb:*:*:table/mytable\"\n   114→    }\n   115→  ]\n   116→}\n   117→```\n   118→\n   119→## Data Source Configuration\n   120→\n   121→To make use of the S3 remote state in another configuration,\n   122→use the [`terraform_remote_state` data source](/terraform/language/state/remote-state-data).\n   123→\n   124→```hcl\n   125→data \"terraform_remote_state\" \"network\" {\n   126→  backend = \"s3\"\n   127→  config = {\n   128→    bucket = \"terraform-state-prod\"\n   129→    key    = \"network/terraform.tfstate\"\n   130→    region = \"us-east-1\"\n   131→  }\n   132→}\n   133→```\n   134→\n   135→The `terraform_remote_state` data source will return all of the root module\n   136→outputs defined in the referenced remote state (but not any outputs from\n   137→nested modules unless they are explicitly output again in the root). An\n   138→example output might look like:\n   139→\n   140→```\n   141→data.terraform_remote_state.network:\n   142→  id = 2016-10-29 01:57:59.780010914 +0000 UTC\n   143→  addresses.# = 2\n   144→  addresses.0 = 52.207.220.222\n   145→  addresses.1 = 54.196.78.166\n   146→  backend = s3\n   147→  config.% = 3\n   148→  config.bucket = terraform-state-prod\n   149→  config.key = network/terraform.tfstate\n   150→  config.region = us-east-1\n   151→  elb_address = web-elb-790251200.us-east-1.elb.amazonaws.com\n   152→  public_subnet_id = subnet-1e05dd33\n   153→```\n   154→\n   155→## Configuration\n   156→\n   157→This backend requires the configuration of the AWS Region and S3 state storage. Other configuration, such as enabling DynamoDB state locking, is optional.\n   158→\n   159→### Credentials and Shared Configuration\n   160→\n   161→!> **Warning:**  We recommend using environment variables to supply credentials and other sensitive data. If you use `-backend-config` or hardcode these values directly in your configuration, Terraform will include these values in both the `.terraform` subdirectory and in plan files. Refer to [Credentials and Sensitive Data](/terraform/language/backend#credentials-and-sensitive-data) for details.\n   162→\n   163→The following configuration is required:\n   164→\n   165→* `region` - (Required) AWS Region of the S3 Bucket and DynamoDB Table (if used). This can also be sourced from the `AWS_DEFAULT_REGION` and `AWS_REGION` environment variables.\n   166→\n   167→The following configuration is optional:\n   168→\n   169→* `use_lockfile` - (Experimental, Optional) Whether to use a lockfile for locking the state file. Defaults to `false`.\n   170→* `access_key` - (Optional) AWS access key. If configured, must also configure `secret_key`. This can also be sourced from the `AWS_ACCESS_KEY_ID` environment variable, AWS shared credentials file (e.g. `~/.aws/credentials`), or AWS shared configuration file (e.g. `~/.aws/config`).\n   171→* `allowed_account_ids` - (Optional) List of allowed AWS account IDs to prevent potential destruction of a live environment. Conflicts with `forbidden_account_ids`.\n   172→* `custom_ca_bundle` - (Optional) File containing custom root and intermediate certificates. Can also be set using the `AWS_CA_BUNDLE` environment variable. Setting ca_bundle in the shared config file is not supported.\n   173→* `ec2_metadata_service_endpoint` - (Optional) Custom endpoint URL for the EC2 Instance Metadata Service (IMDS) API.\n   174→  Can also be set with the `AWS_EC2_METADATA_SERVICE_ENDPOINT` environment variable.\n   175→* `ec2_metadata_service_endpoint_mode` - (Optional) Mode to use in communicating with the metadata service.\n   176→  Valid values are `IPv4` and `IPv6`.\n   177→  Can also be set with the `AWS_EC2_METADATA_SERVICE_ENDPOINT_MODE` environment variable.\n   178→* `forbidden_account_ids` - (Optional) List of forbidden AWS account IDs to prevent potential destruction of a live environment. Conflicts with `allowed_account_ids`.\n   179→* `http_proxy` - (Optional) URL of a proxy to use for HTTP requests when accessing the AWS API.\n   180→  Can also be set using the `HTTP_PROXY` or `http_proxy` environment variables.\n   181→* `https_proxy` - (Optional) URL of a proxy to use for HTTPS requests when accessing the AWS API.\n   182→  Can also be set using the `HTTPS_PROXY` or `https_proxy` environment variables.\n   183→* `iam_endpoint` - (Optional, **Deprecated**) Custom endpoint URL for the AWS Identity and Access Management (IAM) API.\n   184→  Use `endpoints.iam` instead.\n   185→* `insecure` - (Optional) Whether to explicitly allow the backend to perform \"insecure\" SSL requests. If omitted, the default value is `false`.\n   186→* `no_proxy` - (Optional) Comma-separated list of hosts that should not use HTTP or HTTPS proxies.\n   187→  Each value can be one of:\n   188→    * A domain name\n   189→    * An IP address\n   190→    * A CIDR address\n   191→    * An asterisk (`*`), to indicate that no proxying should be performed\n   192→  Domain name and IP address values can also include a port number.\n   193→  Can also be set using the `NO_PROXY` or `no_proxy` environment variables.\n   194→* `max_retries` - (Optional) The maximum number of times an AWS API request is retried on retryable failure. {"type":"assistant","message":{"model":"claude-sonnet-4-6","id":"msg_01XEBc6hvww1eMj3u37qfAh2","type":"message","role":"assistant","content":[{"type":"tool_use","id":"toolu_011HR7TYaQ2544Ht991GEnCU","name":"Write","input":{"file_path":"/workspace/documentation.md","content":"# Terraform v1.9.0 to v1.10.0 Migration Guide\n\n## Overview\n\nTerraform v1.10.0 is a minor release in the stable v1.0 series and honors the [Terraform v1.0 Compatibility Promises](https://developer.hashicorp.com/terraform/language/v1-compatibility-promises). However, this release includes breaking changes in the S3 backend configuration and `moved` block syntax that require manual action before or during upgrade, as well as important behavioral changes to sensitive value propagation and a significant new feature (ephemeral resources and values) that changes how secret data should be handled going forward.\n\n**Key themes:**\n- **Breaking:** S3 backend removes deprecated root-level IAM role assumption attributes\n- **Breaking:** `moved` blocks now require `resource.` prefix for types that conflict with reserved keywords\n- **Behavioral:** Sensitive marks now consistently propagate through conditional and `for` expressions\n- **New:** Ephemeral resources, input variables, and outputs for secret handling without state persistence\n\n---\n\n## Breaking Changes\n\n### 1. S3 Backend: Removal of Root-Level IAM Role Assumption Attributes\n\n#### What Changed and Why\n\nSeveral root-level attributes for IAM role assumption in the S3 backend that were previously deprecated have been **permanently removed** in v1.10.0. These attributes have had block-based replacements since earlier Terraform versions, and the migration was deferred until this release.\n\nThe removed attributes and their replacements are:\n\n| Removed Attribute | Replacement (inside `assume_role` block) |\n|---|---|\n| `role_arn` | `assume_role.role_arn` |\n| `session_name` | `assume_role.session_name` |\n| `external_id` | `assume_role.external_id` |\n| `assume_role_duration_seconds` | `assume_role.duration` |\n| `assume_role_policy` | `assume_role.policy` |\n| `assume_role_policy_arns` | `assume_role.policy_arns` |\n| `assume_role_tags` | `assume_role.tags` |\n| `assume_role_transitive_tag_keys` | `assume_role.transitive_tag_keys` |\n\nNote that `assume_role_duration_seconds` maps to `duration` (not `duration_seconds`) in the block syntax, and accepts a duration string (e.g. `\"1h30m\"`) rather than an integer number of seconds.\n\n#### Before/After Code Examples\n\n**Before (v1.9.0 — using deprecated root-level attributes):**\n\n```hcl\nterraform {\n  backend \"s3\" {\n    bucket       = \"my-terraform-state\"\n    key          = \"prod/terraform.tfstate\"\n    region       = \"us-east-1\"\n\n    # These root-level attributes are REMOVED in v1.10.0\n    role_arn                   = \"arn:aws:iam::123456789012:role/TerraformRole\"\n    session_name               = \"terraform-session\"\n    external_id                = \"my-external-id\"\n    assume_role_duration_seconds = 3600\n    assume_role_policy         = \"{\\\"Version\\\":\\\"2012-10-17\\\",\\\"Statement\\\":[]}\"\n    assume_role_policy_arns    = [\"arn:aws:iam::aws:policy/ReadOnlyAccess\"]\n    assume_role_tags           = { Environment = \"prod\" }\n    assume_role_transitive_tag_keys = [\"Environment\"]\n  }\n}\n```\n\n**After (v1.10.0 — using `assume_role` block):**\n\n```hcl\nterraform {\n  backend \"s3\" {\n    bucket = \"my-terraform-state\"\n    key    = \"prod/terraform.tfstate\"\n    region = \"us-east-1\"\n\n    assume_role = {\n      role_arn               = \"arn:aws:iam::123456789012:role/TerraformRole\"\n      session_name           = \"terraform-session\"\n      external_id            = \"my-external-id\"\n      duration               = \"1h\"   # Note: string format, not seconds integer\n      policy                 = \"{\\\"Version\\\":\\\"2012-10-17\\\",\\\"Statement\\\":[]}\"\n      policy_arns            = [\"arn:aws:iam::aws:policy/ReadOnlyAccess\"]\n      tags                   = { Environment = \"prod\" }\n      transitive_tag_keys    = [\"Environment\"]\n    }\n  }\n}\n```\n\n#### Step-by-Step Migration Instructions\n\n1. **Identify affected configurations:** Search all your Terraform configuration files for any `backend \"s3\"` blocks that contain root-level `role_arn`, `session_name`, `external_id`, `assume_role_duration_seconds`, `assume_role_policy`, `assume_role_policy_arns`, `assume_role_tags`, or `assume_role_transitive_tag_keys` attributes.\n\n   ```bash\n   grep -rn \"role_arn\\|session_name\\|external_id\\|assume_role_duration_seconds\\|assume_role_policy\\|assume_role_tags\\|assume_role_transitive_tag_keys\" . --include=\"*.tf\" --include=\"*.tfbackend\"\n   ```\n\n2. **Update each backend configuration:** Move all the matching root-level attributes inside an `assume_role = { ... }` block. Convert `assume_role_duration_seconds` (integer) to `duration` (duration string, e.g. `3600` seconds becomes `\"1h\"`).\n\n3. **Handle partial configurations:** If your backend uses `-backend-config` flags or partial configuration files (`.tfbackend`), update those files as well. The block syntax must be used in all forms.\n\n4. **Re-initialize:** After updating the configuration, run `terraform init` to validate the new backend configuration:\n\n   ```bash\n   terraform init\n   ```\n\n5. **Verify:** Confirm that `terraform plan` runs without errors.\n\n#### Common Pitfalls\n\n- **Duration format change:** `assume_role_duration_seconds` accepted an integer (e.g. `3600`), but `duration` in the `assume_role` block requires a duration string (e.g. `\"1h\"`, `\"90m\"`, `\"1h30m\"`). Forgetting this conversion will cause a validation error.\n- **Partial configuration files:** Backend configuration supplied via `-backend-config` files must also be updated to use the block syntax.\n- **Multiple `assume_role` blocks:** The `assume_role` argument supports multiple values in v1.10.0 — roles are assumed in order. If you were chaining role assumptions, you can now express this as a list.\n\n---\n\n### 2. `moved` Blocks: Required `resource.` Prefix for Reserved Type Names\n\n#### What Changed and Why\n\nIn v1.10.0, `moved` blocks now correctly parse resource addresses using the same rules as the rest of the Terraform language. This means reserved keywords and top-level block types (such as `local`, `each`, `self`, `count`, `path`, `module`, `var`, `data`, `resource`, `provider`, `terraform`, `check`, and similar) are now recognized as keywords rather than resource type names when they appear as the first segment of an address in `from` or `to` fields.\n\nIf you have a resource with a **type name** that matches one of these reserved words (e.g., `resource \"local\" \"config\" {}`), you must now use the `resource.` prefix in any `moved` block that references it.\n\n#### Before/After Code Examples\n\n**Scenario:** A resource with type `local` (an unusual but valid type name from a custom provider).\n\n**Before (v1.9.0 — worked, now errors in v1.10.0):**\n\n```hcl\n# Resource definition\nresource \"local\" \"config\" {\n  # ...\n}\n\n# This moved block previously parsed \"local.config\" as resource type \"local\", name \"config\"\nmoved {\n  from = local.config\n  to   = local.settings\n}\n```\n\n**After (v1.10.0 — must use `resource.` prefix):**\n\n```hcl\n# Resource definition\nresource \"local\" \"config\" {\n  # ...\n}\n\n# Must now explicitly qualify with \"resource.\" when the type matches a keyword\nmoved {\n  from = resource.local.config\n  to   = resource.local.settings\n}\n```\n\n#### Step-by-Step Migration Instructions\n\n1. **Identify affected `moved` blocks:** Search for `moved` blocks in your configurations and check whether the resource type name in the `from` or `to` fields matches any reserved keyword.\n\n   Reserved keywords include (but are not limited to): `local`, `locals`, `each`, `self`, `count`, `path`, `module`, `var`, `data`, `resource`, `provider`, `terraform`, `check`, `import`, `removed`, `moved`.\n\n   ```bash\n   grep -rn \"moved {\" . --include=\"*.tf\" -A 5\n   ```\n\n2. **Check for conflicts:** For each `moved` block, examine the `from` and `to` addresses. If the first segment (the type name) matches a reserved word, add the `resource.` prefix.\n\n3. **Test with `terraform validate`:** Run `terraform validate` after updating to confirm there are no remaining errors:\n\n   ```bash\n   terraform validate\n   ```\n\n4. **Run a plan to confirm:** Execute `terraform plan` to verify that Terraform correctly identifies the moved resources and proposes no unexpected changes:\n\n   ```bash\n   terraform plan\n   ```\n\n#### Common Pitfalls\n\n- **Custom providers with generic type names:** Some older or community providers use type names like `local`, `data`, or `module` which can conflict. These configurations need the `resource.` qualifier.\n- **Module-level `moved` blocks:** This applies to `moved` blocks in both root modules and child modules. Check all modules in your configuration.\n- **The `resource.` prefix is always valid:** Even in cases where the prefix was not previously required, adding `resource.` to a resource address in a `moved` block is valid in all Terraform v1.x versions. If you are unsure whether a type name conflicts, adding the prefix is safe.\n\n---\n\n## Behavioral Changes\n\n### 3. Sensitive Value Handling: Consistent Mark Propagation in Expressions\n\n#### What Changed and Why\n\nPrior to v1.10.0, there was a bug where sensitive marks (the internal mechanism that causes Terraform to redact values shown as `(sensitive value)`) were **incorrectly dropped** in certain conditional expressions (`condition ? true_val : false_val`) and `for` expressions when one of the branches or elements involved an unknown value. This could cause two problems:\n\n1. **Mark loss:** A value that should have been marked sensitive was silently treated as non-sensitive, potentially exposing secret data in plan output.\n2. **Erroneous plan changes:** Sensitive marks were sometimes incorrectly transferred to unrelated nested resource values, causing Terraform to plan spurious changes.\n\nIn v1.10.0, both issues are fixed. Sensitive marks now propagate correctly through all expression types. The result is:\n\n- Expressions that involve a sensitive value will consistently produce a sensitive result.\n- You may see **previously hidden** plan changes become visible now that incorrect mark propagation no longer masks them.\n- Some expressions that previously appeared non-sensitive may now correctly appear as `(sensitive value)` in plan output.\n\n#### When Sensitive Marks Are Now Preserved\n\nIn v1.10.0, the sensitive mark is preserved in all of the following cases (some of which previously dropped the mark):\n\n```hcl\n# If var.secret is sensitive, the result of a conditional expression\n# is now consistently marked sensitive, even when one branch is unknown.\nlocal.value = var.some_condition ? var.secret : \"default\"\n\n# In for expressions, sensitivity is properly propagated\nlocal.filtered = [for item in var.sensitive_list : item if item != \"\"]\n\n# Conditional with unknown condition is now correctly sensitive\nlocal.result = var.unknown_flag ? var.secret : var.other_secret\n```\n\n#### How to Use `nonsensitive()` to Override When Needed\n\nIf the consistent propagation causes `(sensitive value)` to appear where you know the resulting value is safe to expose, use the `nonsensitive()` function to explicitly unwrap the sensitivity mark:\n\n```hcl\n# Before v1.10.0: this might have accidentally exposed the value as non-sensitive\n# After v1.10.0: the result is correctly marked sensitive\nlocal.public_info = var.is_public ? var.api_endpoint : var.secret_endpoint\n\n# If you are certain the output is safe to display, use nonsensitive()\noutput \"safe_endpoint\" {\n  value = nonsensitive(var.is_public ? var.api_endpoint : var.secret_endpoint)\n}\n```\n\n**Only use `nonsensitive()` when you are certain the value does not contain sensitive data.** Misuse can expose secret values in plan output, state files, and logs.\n\n#### Migration Steps\n\n1. **Run `terraform plan` after upgrading** and review any new `(sensitive value)` annotations or any plan changes that were previously hidden. These may indicate legitimate changes that were previously masked.\n\n2. **Review output blocks** that reference conditional expressions involving sensitive variables. If the output is now unexpectedly marked sensitive, you have two options:\n   - Declare the output as `sensitive = true` to explicitly acknowledge it.\n   - Restructure the expression to avoid mixing sensitive and non-sensitive paths, or use `nonsensitive()` if appropriate.\n\n3. **Review resource configurations** that produce unexpected plan changes. If plan changes disappear after upgrade, they may have been artifacts of the previous incorrect mark propagation behavior. Conversely, new plan changes may reflect previously masked real differences.\n\n#### Common Pitfalls\n\n- **`nonsensitive()` does not remove sensitivity from state:** The `nonsensitive()` function only affects plan/apply output display. Sensitive values are still stored in state unless you use ephemeral values (see below).\n- **Unexpected plan diffs:** If you see new plan changes after upgrading that relate to sensitive resource attributes, investigate whether the change is real or was previously masked. Consider using `terraform show` or `terraform state show` to examine the current state.\n\n---\n\n## New Features with Migration Impact\n\n### 4. Ephemeral Resources and Values\n\nTerraform v1.10.0 introduces **ephemeral resources, ephemeral input variables, and ephemeral outputs** as a new mechanism for handling secrets that should never be persisted to state or plan files. This is a public beta feature and the workflows may evolve in future releases.\n\n> **Note:** Ephemeral resources, variables, and outputs are in public beta in v1.10.0. Provider support for ephemeral resources requires providers that implement the new ephemeral resource type.\n\n#### What Is Ephemeral?\n\nEphemeral values exist only in memory during a single Terraform operation (plan or apply). Unlike `sensitive` values, which are stored in state (just redacted in output), **ephemeral values are never written to state files, plan files, or logs**.\n\nUse ephemeral values for:\n- Short-lived authentication tokens (e.g. Vault leases, OIDC tokens)\n- Database passwords that should not persist in state\n- API keys that rotate frequently\n- Any credential that should exist only for the duration of a Terraform run\n\n#### Ephemeral Input Variables\n\nMark an input variable as ephemeral to prevent it from being stored in state:\n\n```hcl\nvariable \"db_password\" {\n  type      = string\n  ephemeral = true\n  sensitive = true  # Also mark sensitive to suppress CLI output\n}\n```\n\nEphemeral variables can only be referenced in:\n- Other ephemeral variables\n- Local values (which become implicitly ephemeral)\n- Ephemeral resources\n- Ephemeral outputs\n- Provider configuration blocks\n- Provisioner and connection blocks\n\n**If you reference an ephemeral variable in a non-ephemeral context, Terraform will return an error.**\n\n#### Ephemeral Outputs (Child Modules Only)\n\nMark an output in a **child module** as ephemeral to pass secrets between modules without persisting them:\n\n```hcl\n# modules/db/main.tf\noutput \"connection_string\" {\n  value     = \"postgres://${var.username}:${var.db_password}@${var.host}:5432/${var.database}\"\n  ephemeral = true\n  sensitive = true\n}\n```\n\n> **Important:** Ephemeral outputs are only supported in child modules. You cannot mark a root module output as `ephemeral`.\n\n#### Ephemeral Resources (Provider Support Required)\n\nProviders that support ephemeral resources expose them via a new `ephemeral` block type. Ephemeral resources are opened, optionally renewed, and closed during each Terraform operation — their data is never stored in state:\n\n```hcl\n# Retrieve a secret from AWS Secrets Manager without storing it in state\nephemeral \"aws_secretsmanager_secret_version\" \"db_credentials\" {\n  secret_id = data.aws_db_instance.example.master_user_secret[0].secret_arn\n}\n\nlocals {\n  credentials = jsondecode(ephemeral.aws_secretsmanager_secret_version.db_credentials.secret_string)\n}\n\nprovider \"postgresql\" {\n  host     = data.aws_db_instance.example.address\n  port     = data.aws_db_instance.example.port\n  username = local.credentials[\"username\"]\n  password = local.credentials[\"password\"]\n}\n```\n\n#### The `ephemeralasnull` Function\n\nA new built-in function `ephemeralasnull()` converts ephemeral values to `null`, allowing you to output or use the non-ephemeral parts of a mixed object:\n\n```hcl\nvariable \"session_token\" {\n  type      = string\n  ephemeral = true\n}\n\nvariable \"environment\" {\n  type    = string\n  default = \"production\"\n}\n\nlocals {\n  config = {\n    environment   = var.environment\n    session_token = var.session_token  # ephemeral\n  }\n}\n\n# Output only the non-ephemeral parts; session_token becomes null\noutput \"safe_config\" {\n  value = ephemeralasnull(local.config)\n}\n```\n\n#### Migration Impact: Sensitive vs. Ephemeral\n\nBefore v1.10.0, the only way to protect secrets was `sensitive = true`, which hides values in output but **still stores them in state**. Starting with v1.10.0, you have a stronger guarantee with ephemeral values:\n\n| Property | `sensitive = true` | `ephemeral = true` |\n|---|---|---|\n| Hidden in plan/apply output | Yes | Yes (never shown) |\n| Stored in state file | **Yes** | **No** |\n| Stored in plan file | **Yes** | **No** |\n| Available in next run from state | Yes | No (must be re-provided) |\n| Can be used in regular resources | Yes | No (ephemeral contexts only) |\n\n**When to use which:**\n- Use `sensitive` for values that must persist in state (e.g., resource IDs that happen to be sensitive)\n- Use `ephemeral` for credentials, tokens, and passwords that should never touch state\n\n#### IAM and Bucket Policy Updates for S3 Locking\n\nWhen enabling S3 native state locking (`use_lockfile = true`), the lock file is placed alongside the state file with a `.tflock` extension. Update your S3 bucket policies and IAM policies to include permissions for the lock file path:\n\n```json\n{\n  \"Version\": \"2012-10-17\",\n  \"Statement\": [\n    {\n      \"Effect\": \"Allow\",\n      \"Action\": [\"s3:GetObject\", \"s3:PutObject\", \"s3:DeleteObject\"],\n      \"Resource\": [\n        \"arn:aws:s3:::mybucket/path/to/my/key\",\n        \"arn:aws:s3:::mybucket/path/to/my/key.tflock\"\n      ]\n    }\n  ]\n}\n```\n\n#### Opting Into S3 Native State Locking\n\nS3 native locking is opt-in and experimental in v1.10.0. To enable it:\n\n```hcl\nterraform {\n  backend \"s3\" {\n    bucket   = \"my-terraform-state\"\n    key      = \"prod/terraform.tfstate\"\n    region   = \"us-east-1\"\n\n    # Experimental S3 native locking (opt-in)\n    use_lockfile = true\n\n    # DynamoDB locking can run alongside S3 locking during transition\n    dynamodb_table = \"terraform-state-lock\"\n  }\n}\n```\n\nWhen both `use_lockfile` and `dynamodb_table` are configured, **both locks must be acquired** before an operation proceeds. This allows a gradual migration from DynamoDB-only locking to S3-only locking.\n\n> **Note:** In a future Terraform minor version, DynamoDB-based locking will be deprecated and S3 native locking will become the standard. Plan your migration path accordingly.\n\n---\n\n## Testing Your Migration\n\nFollow these steps to validate a successful upgrade from v1.9.0 to v1.10.0:\n\n### 1. Update Your Terraform Version\n\n```bash\n# Verify the installed version\nterraform version\n# Should show: Terraform v1.10.x\n```\n\nIf using a version constraint in your configuration, update it:\n\n```hcl\nterraform {\n  required_version = \">= 1.10.0\"\n}\n```\n\n### 2. Address Breaking Changes First\n\nBefore running any Terraform commands:\n\n1. Update all S3 backend configurations to remove deprecated root-level IAM attributes (see [S3 Backend Changes](#1-s3-backend-removal-of-root-level-iam-role-assumption-attributes))\n2. Update any `moved` blocks that reference resource types with reserved keyword names (see [`moved` Block Changes](#2-moved-blocks-required-resource-prefix-for-reserved-type-names))\n\n### 3. Re-Initialize\n\n```bash\nterraform init -upgrade\n```\n\nThis re-downloads providers and validates the backend configuration.\n\n### 4. Validate Configuration\n\n```bash\nterraform validate\n```\n\nAddress any errors or warnings before proceeding. Warnings about deprecated attributes (like `dynamodb_table` or `shared_credentials_file`) can be resolved at your discretion but are not blocking.\n\n### 5. Run a Plan and Review\n\n```bash\nterraform plan -out=upgrade-plan.tfplan\n```\n\nReview the plan carefully for:\n\n- Any **new `(sensitive value)` annotations** — these are now correctly marked; verify they are expected\n- Any **new resource changes** that were not expected — these may have been masked by the previous mark propagation bug\n- Ensure no resource **replacements** are proposed unexpectedly\n\n### 6. Apply the Plan\n\nIf the plan looks correct:\n\n```bash\nterraform apply upgrade-plan.tfplan\n```\n\n### 7. Verify State\n\n```bash\nterraform show\n```\n\nConfirm that the state reflects the expected infrastructure state.\n\n### 8. Test Sensitive Value Behavior (if applicable)\n\nIf your configuration uses sensitive variables with conditional expressions, verify the behavior:\n\n```bash\nterraform plan -var=\"secret_value=test-value\"\n```\n\nConfirm that sensitive values appear as `(sensitive value)` in the output where expected.\n\n---\n\n## Rollback Guidance\n\nIf you encounter issues after upgrading to v1.10.0 and need to revert:\n\n### Prerequisites Before Upgrading\n\nBefore upgrading, take these precautions:\n\n1. **Back up your state files:** Copy your state files to a safe location before running `terraform apply` with v1.10.0.\n\n   ```bash\n   # For local state\n   cp terraform.tfstate terraform.tfstate.v1.9-backup\n\n   # For S3 backend — enable versioning if not already enabled\n   aws s3api put-bucket-versioning \\\n     --bucket my-terraform-state \\\n     --versioning-configuration Status=Enabled\n   ```\n\n2. **Record your current state version:** Note the state serial number with `terraform show -json | jq '.values.root_module'` before upgrading.\n\n### Rolling Back the Terraform Binary\n\n1. Reinstall Terraform v1.9.x:\n\n   ```bash\n   # Using tfenv\n   tfenv install 1.9.8\n   tfenv use 1.9.8\n\n   # Or download directly from releases.hashicorp.com\n   ```\n\n2. Revert your configuration changes (restore deprecated attribute usage in S3 backend, remove `resource.` prefixes from `moved` blocks if needed).\n\n3. Run `terraform init` to re-initialize with the older version.\n\n### Rolling Back S3 Native Locking\n\nIf you enabled `use_lockfile = true` and need to roll back:\n\n1. Ensure no Terraform operations are in progress (check for `.tflock` files in your S3 bucket).\n2. Remove `use_lockfile = true` from your backend configuration.\n3. Run `terraform init` to re-initialize.\n4. If any `.tflock` files remain in S3 from a failed operation, remove them manually before running Terraform again:\n\n   ```bash\n   aws s3 rm s3://my-bucket/path/to/state.tflock\n   ```\n\n### Rolling Back State If Needed\n\nIf a `terraform apply` with v1.10.0 produced unexpected changes:\n\n1. Use `terraform state` commands to inspect and correct state:\n\n   ```bash\n   terraform state list\n   terraform state show <resource_address>\n   ```\n\n2. For S3 backends with versioning enabled, restore a previous state version:\n\n   ```bash\n   aws s3api list-object-versions \\\n     --bucket my-terraform-state \\\n     --prefix path/to/my/key\n\n   aws s3api get-object \\\n     --bucket my-terraform-state \\\n     --key path/to/my/key \\\n     --version-id <version-id> \\\n     terraform.tfstate.restored\n   ```\n\n3. Push the restored state:\n\n   ```bash\n   terraform state push terraform.tfstate.restored\n   ```\n\n### Important Rollback Caveats\n\n- If you used ephemeral resources or variables during the upgraded run, rolling back to v1.9.0 means those features will no longer be available. You must refactor configurations back to use `sensitive` variables.\n- The `assume_role` block syntax for the S3 backend is supported in v1.9.x. If you have already migrated to the block syntax, you do not need to revert to the deprecated root-level attributes.\n- S3 native locking (`.tflock` files) is only understood by Terraform v1.10.0+. If rolling back to v1.9.x, ensure any lock files are cleaned up first.\n\n---\n\n## Summary of Changes at a Glance\n\n| Change | Type | Action Required |\n|--------|------|-----------------|\n| S3 backend root-level IAM attributes removed | **Breaking** | Must migrate to `assume_role` block |\n| `moved` blocks require `resource.` prefix for reserved type names | **Breaking** | Update `moved` blocks if affected |\n| Sensitive marks propagate correctly in conditional/for expressions | Behavioral (bug fix) | Review plan output for new sensitive annotations or unexpected changes |\n| Ephemeral resources, variables, outputs (beta) | New Feature | Optional; adopt for improved secret management |\n| S3 native state locking via `use_lockfile` (experimental) | New Feature | Optional; consider for migration away from DynamoDB locking |\n| `ephemeralasnull()` function | New Feature | Optional; use with ephemeral values |\n\nFor further help, see the [official Terraform upgrade guide](https://developer.hashicorp.com/terraform/language/upgrade-guides) or start a discussion in the [Terraform community forum](https://discuss.hashicorp.com/c/terraform-core).\n"},"caller":{"type":"direct"}}],"stop_reason":null,"stop_sequence":null,"usage":{"input_tokens":1,"cache_creation_input_tokens":2212,"cache_read_input_tokens":126159,"cache_creation":{"ephemeral_5m_input_tokens":0,"ephemeral_1h_input_tokens":2212},"output_tokens":2,"service_tier":"standard","inference_geo":"not_available"},"context_management":null},"parent_tool_use_id":null,"session_id":"e3bf2e19-2a05-4652-9e5a-3579b8d5e1b3","uuid":"544426cf-b472-42ed-81aa-17911670d253"}
